# Buyer-first data-center research

Research cutoff: 2026-10-29

Reverse the sequence: obtain the developer’s current requirement, use the tailored search brief, validate one specific offering, then test affordable transferable control. This is a research and call-priority dataset; no new site has passed investment or control gates.

## Scope and probability method

The modeled event is that the named developer funds and begins physical construction of a first permanent data-center phase, or a major incremental conversion/expansion, on the identified offering by October 29, 2031. A purchase, announcement, land clearing or existing facility operation alone does not count. Actual buyer-specific construction probability is not yet estimable from public evidence.

### Assumptions

- User-specified condition: adequate technically deliverable power capacity for the buyer-approved first phase is demonstrated. None of these sites currently has utility-verified available MW in this research. Facility MW and critical IT MW are kept distinct; no fixed PUE conversion is assumed.
- Power suitability does not silently waive power price, upgrade allocation, collateral, tariff, redundancy, exact service date or transferability. Those commercial and scheduling issues remain in the execution gates unless the buyer separately accepts them.
- Each numerical scenario shows three subjective conditional inputs: buyer selection given power × non-power feasibility given selection × financed construction start given preceding gates. This is a conditional chain, not multiplication of independent marginal risks.
- Low/base/high outputs are products of the displayed assumption inputs, rounded to whole percentages. They are transparent planning cases, not measured rates, confidence intervals or statistically calibrated forecasts. You can change the base inputs in the site card to see sensitivity.
- The first-phase load in each card is a hypothesis to validate with the buyer. It is not an available-MW claim, engineering design or developer mandate. All other information is held at the public-evidence stage.
- Buyer identity, customers, competing owned inventory, customer latency, layout, permits, cooling/water, fiber construction, funding, seller cooperation and economics remain material. Existing facilities are modeled only for incremental build or major conversion.
- Hinds, the unavailable TRIC listing and the auction adjacency hypothesis receive no numerical scenario until their stated status/identity gates are resolved. Archived or rejected leads do not acquire a forecast merely because power is assumed.

### Limitations

- 100 profiles are not 100 confirmed buyers. The directory includes operating developers, owner-builders, powered-land/master developers and nine international watch profiles. Roles and parent/JV links are disclosed.
- No developer has reviewed these site matches. Sources include published intake criteria, regulatory filings, project announcements, company pages and broker/EDC offerings. Project precedents are not universal purchasing requirements.
- No negative-project denominator, comparable conversion cohort or private buying mandate exists in this dataset. These subjective ranges can be substantially wrong; they must not be used as a land valuation or justification for spending.
- A buyer may build yet never buy a Syncranicity option. Sale price is not option cost. Assignment rights, seller authority, utility study reliance/transfer and compensation have not been established for any new lead.
- Matches share risks and some companies share parents or JVs. Do not add the percentages or calculate an at-least-one-buyer probability as if the opportunities were independent. Site counts are marketed offering records: the older Ovilla Road and Project Venus records may overlap and are flagged for parcel reconciliation.
- Availability, asking prices and published plans can change. A listing or EDC record is evidence of marketing, not seller confirmation. APNs shown are reported identifiers, not a title or boundary certification.
- The existing <$100,000 founder capital constraint, $5,000 learning cap and $10,000 soft-control cap remain unchanged. This research authorizes no payment, bid, reservation or outreach.

## 100 developer profiles

### 1. Dataprana / Prana Energy

**Role:** Developer / operator / powered-site originator · **Lane:** Mid-scale / urban AI

**Parent / shared interests:** Dataprana; Prana Energy is its land and power division, not a second independent buyer

Power-first mid-scale developer with an explicit search focus close to Syncranicity’s target. Its record includes mining infrastructure as well as developing AI/HPC opportunities; qualifying the actual end use matters because mining interruptibility and networking do not automatically satisfy AI requirements.

**Geography:** SPP; MISO; ERCOT; Texas; South Carolina; Other US expansion markets

**Scale:** Current land-and-energy page targets 10–50 MW; September 2026 company release describes Texas parcels of 10–75 MW. Basis is not expressly IT load and must be clarified.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** Public land division targets scalable substation-adjacent sites in SPP, MISO and ERCOT, emphasizing 10–50 MW and a claimed 12–18-month greenfield-to-service delivery model. [Source](https://dataprana.io/land-and-energy/)
- **project evidence:** September 2026 hiring announcement expands power origination, land acquisition and engineering and describes projects aimed at late-2026/2027 energization. [Source](https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/09/14/3360924/0/en/dataprana-welcomes-land-and-power-executives-amid-us-data-center-market-expansion.html)

**What changes the search**

- Explicit mid-scale focus and land-acquisition function make this more actionable than a megacampus-only platform.
- SPP/MISO/ERCOT are market filters, not proof of exact serving utility, available MW or tariff economics.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: the key differentiator is a dated, studyable service path with manageable upgrades, rather than maximizing parcel area.
- Inference: ask whether a tract is sought for AI colocation, powered land resale, mining or BESS before matching its network and uptime characteristics.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: power timing much slower than the marketed delivery model.
- Inference: economically viable mining site but inadequate AI-grade fiber/reliability.

**Questions to validate**

- Is the current target 10–50 MW facility load, utility contract demand or IT load?
- What maximum all-in power price, curtailment and upgrade cost are acceptable for the intended workload?
- At which milestone would you acquire an assignable option rather than source the land directly?

**Transaction route:** Owns/develops land and power infrastructure; direct partnering inquiry is public. Assignment acquisition, reimbursement and seller-financed land terms are unconfirmed.

**Acquisition signal:** Dedicated acquisition hires and current public land brief indicate active sourcing; no interest in Hinds or any particular candidate has been established.

**Search brief:** Prioritize exact parcels in the disclosed grids with 10–50 MW staged delivery potential, economical network access, acceptable community/use path and enough expansion land. Hinds is a conditional regional lead; the exact utility/ISO territory and offered tract remain unresolved.

1 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified. 

**Sources**

- [Land and energy criteria](https://dataprana.io/land-and-energy/) — Dataprana; undated; checked 2026-10-29
- [Land and power executive expansion](https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/09/14/3360924/0/en/dataprana-welcomes-land-and-power-executives-amid-us-data-center-market-expansion.html) — Dataprana company release; 2026-09-14; checked 2026-10-29
- [Company profile](https://dataprana.io/about-us/) — Dataprana; undated; checked 2026-10-29

### 2. metrobloks

**Role:** Developer / operator · **Lane:** Mid-scale / urban AI

**Parent / shared interests:** Metrobloks; Lincoln Property Company JV overlaps Lincoln-related exposure

Urban AI and inference developer with one of the clearest public property-submission briefs. This is a strong reverse-engineering starting point because acreage, floor-area ratio, height, exchange distance, power and deal flexibility are actually published, rather than deduced from one announcement.

**Geography:** Major US metros; Kansas City; Urban European metros

**Scale:** Published target 5–20 developable acres; ability to procure 20+ MW. Published power basis is not labeled IT. Kansas City example is approximately 30 acres, showing stated preferences can admit exceptions.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** Prefers 5–20 developable acres, FAR at least 0.5, commercial/industrial zoning, 30-foot minimum height with 60+ feet preferred, urban road/fiber access, and ability to procure 20+ MW. [Source](https://www.metrobloks.com/realestate)
- **published requirement:** Published site criteria call for major metro hubs and less than 15 miles from a local internet exchange; landowner JVs and flexible development escrows are explicitly considered. [Source](https://www.metrobloks.com/realestate)
- **project evidence:** April 2026 Lincoln JV acquired roughly 30 acres in Liberty, Missouri, within ten miles of GPC Missouri IXP. [Source](https://lpc.com/press-releases/metrobloks-and-lincoln-property-company-announce-joint-venture-to-develop-ai-ready-data-centers-launching-in-kansas-city-metro/)

**What changes the search**

- Direct site intake specifies precise location and size.
- Explicit landowner-JV openness and development escrow preference align with conditional-control sourcing.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: map the specific exchange and available fiber paths before spending on a substation-proximity study; the urban latency constraint can eliminate rural sites even with power.
- Inference: split residential zoning or restrictive FAR may defeat apparent acreage fit and should be resolved before an option fee.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Published-screen mismatch: remote geography, exchange distance or unsuitable height/zoning.
- Inference: a 10-acre tract is not enough if setbacks, streams or easements remove most buildable land.

**Questions to validate**

- Which metros are open for new submissions now?
- Does <15 miles refer to straight-line, road or routed fiber distance and which IXP qualifies?
- Will you acquire an assignable land contract, and how much power/entitlement proof is required first?

**Transaction route:** Direct acquisition, development-oriented escrow and landowner JV publicly offered; founder assignment rights and any premium remain negotiation matters.

**Acquisition signal:** Published land submission channel plus a new Kansas City development partnership, not just passive portfolio growth.

**Search brief:** Screen 5–20-acre commercial/industrial infill in major US metros, including Kansas City, against the <15-mile IXP test, permitted FAR/height, developable acreage and a 20+ MW path.

3 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified. 

**Sources**

- [Real estate acquisition criteria and intake](https://www.metrobloks.com/realestate) — Metrobloks; undated; checked 2026-10-29
- [Kansas City JV](https://lpc.com/press-releases/metrobloks-and-lincoln-property-company-announce-joint-venture-to-develop-ai-ready-data-centers-launching-in-kansas-city-metro/) — Lincoln Property Company; 2026-04-13; checked 2026-10-29

### 3. AIB Data Centers

**Role:** Developer / operator · **Lane:** Mid-scale / urban AI

**Parent / shared interests:** AIB Data Centers Inc.; formerly BlockchAIn Digital Infrastructure

Emerging AI/HPC developer targeting manageable capacity increments and utility-first execution. A documented Texas acquisition is unusually relevant because it combined fee-simple real estate, project-company rights and contracted electrical service. It does not demonstrate that AIB pays the same price for an unsupported raw-land option.

**Geography:** Carolinas; Dallas–Fort Worth; Minnesota; United States

**Scale:** Current website emphasizes 40–90 MW centers. Investor material describes sites at 150 MW or less with 10 MW modular halls; treat the former as current marketing focus, not a universal ceiling. Contracted utility capacity is distinct from IT load.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** SEC filing states significant commitments require deliverable utility capacity supported by an executed service agreement/PPA or equivalent, land control and a defined interconnection path. [Source](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2070542/000121390026089151/ea0301093-10q_aibdata.htm)
- **project evidence:** September 2026 transaction acquired about 29.385 acres through linked land and LLC-interest purchases, combining 15 MW existing service with 40 MW future primary service under a facilities agreement. [Source](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2070542/000121390026099305/ea0305122-8k_aib.htm)
- **published strategy / product:** Current site describes 40–90 MW standardized developments and five marketed sites. [Source](https://www.aib.us/)

**What changes the search**

- Public SEC precedent permits examination of the actual acquisition mechanism.
- The linked transaction includes utility security and deferred payments; gross consideration is neither an assignment fee nor seller profit.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: land plus assignable utility rights is more saleable than power-proximity speculation; exact documentary rights are part of the asset.
- Inference: deferred consideration creates collection/timing risk even when a buyer accepts the project, so takeout price and cash paid at closing must be separated.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Published-policy mismatch: no contract-backed utility path before significant capital.
- Inference: transaction cannot transfer land and electrical rights together.

**Questions to validate**

- Would a 20–30 MW first phase qualify if expansion reaches 40–90 MW?
- Must fee title vest at closing or can you buy an option/SPV first?
- What payment is immediate, what is deferred, and who funds utility security and land exercise?

**Transaction route:** Verified fee-simple purchase and LLC membership purchase with deferred, secured consideration. A standalone assignment of Syncranicity’s option is not verified.

**Acquisition signal:** Completed September 2026 acquisition and current site pipeline show activity; finance, tenant commitments and execution remain separately underwritten.

**Search brief:** Source 40–90 MW utility-rights packages near targeted US markets, or seek an explicit smaller-phase exception. Require evidence of service agreement transferability, upgrade liabilities and closing mechanics.

2 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified. 

**Sources**

- [2026 Q2 Form 10-Q](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2070542/000121390026089151/ea0301093-10q_aibdata.htm) — AIB / SEC; 2026; checked 2026-10-29
- [Texas acquisition Form 8-K](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2070542/000121390026099305/ea0305122-8k_aib.htm) — AIB / SEC; 2026-09; checked 2026-10-29
- [Current platform and sites](https://www.aib.us/) — AIB; undated; checked 2026-10-29
- [Investor platform description](https://invest.aib.us/) — AIB; undated; checked 2026-10-29

### 4. RadiusDC

**Role:** network-dense urban/edge developer/operator · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** RadiusDC platform; Nashville development backed by Blue Owl

Targets underserved metropolitan markets with existing carrier ecosystems and complementary new campuses. For RadiusDC, network density, urban demand and expansion around an operating business are core economic inputs.

**Geography:** Denver; Miami; Nashville; Indianapolis; Atlanta; Phoenix

**Scale:** Nashville precedent: roughly 12 acres for 12 MW critical power. Phoenix announced expansion targets approximately 26 MW total critical IT power; platform utility-capacity figures are larger and should not be conflated.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** Published platform targets supply-constrained US metropolitan markets, direct multiple-carrier access and low latency to end users/cloud/enterprise networks. [Source](https://www.radius-dc.com/platform)
- **project evidence:** Phoenix transaction combines an existing data center, interconnection infrastructure and development rights for an additional 18 MW critical capacity from 1H 2028. [Source](https://www.radius-dc.com/newsroom-and-events/radiusdc-to-acquire-phoenixnaps-phoenix-data-center-and-colocation-business)
- **project evidence:** Nashville ground-up project uses a roughly 12-acre site in the urban core near multiple local and long-haul carrier routes. [Source](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/radiusdc-expands-its-footprint-with-a-new-data-center-colocation-facility-in-nashville-tn-302393935.html)
- **project evidence:** The Denver hub at 1500 Champa is fully leased and RadiusDC says it is actively evaluating expansion opportunities in that market. [Source](https://www.radius-dc.com/insights-resources/denver-colorado)

**What changes the search**

- Explicit metropolitan supply-gap strategy.
- Distinguishes network-dense carrier hotels from purpose-built edge campuses; prospects should be tagged accordingly.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: a fiber-rich urban industrial parcel can outrank a much larger rural site even with identical power.
- Inference: existing customers, cross-connect income and a carrier ecosystem reduce market-entry risk in an acquisition.
- Inference: develop a 10–30 MW urban concept and verify the actual fiber topology rather than pitching a remote hyperscale acreage story.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: weak carrier ecosystem despite adequate MW.
- Inference: no evidence of local enterprise/edge demand or inability to create a contiguous expansion.

**Questions to validate**

- Which metro has unmet colocation/inference demand after current expansions?
- Do you need a stabilized carrier hotel with growth rights, or a new 10–30 MW campus?

**Transaction route:** Operational acquisitions with development rights and ground-up new campuses are both documented. Option assignment appetite not disclosed.

**Acquisition signal:** March 2026 Phoenix acquisition agreement is a current strategic signal; the release itself says closing was expected subject to conditions, so do not infer completed closing solely from it. Current Denver page explicitly states active evaluation of expansion opportunities, though no interest in the Thornton offering is established.

**Search brief:** Search urban industrial or carrier-hotel-adjacent sites in its six identified metros. Existing shell plus credible expansion can be more attractive than raw acreage. Prioritize a technically viable satellite linked to 1500 Champa; its Denver page says the hub is fully leased and expansion is being evaluated.

1 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified. 

**Sources**

- [Platform selection criteria](https://www.radius-dc.com/platform) — RadiusDC; undated; checked 2026-10-29
- [Phoenix acquisition agreement](https://www.radius-dc.com/newsroom-and-events/radiusdc-to-acquire-phoenixnaps-phoenix-data-center-and-colocation-business) — RadiusDC; 2026-03-12; checked 2026-10-29
- [Nashville development announcement](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/radiusdc-expands-its-footprint-with-a-new-data-center-colocation-facility-in-nashville-tn-302393935.html) — RadiusDC via PR Newswire; 2025-03-06; checked 2026-10-29
- [Denver hub and active expansion evaluation](https://www.radius-dc.com/insights-resources/denver-colorado) — RadiusDC; undated; checked 2026-10-29

### 5. DC BLOX

**Role:** regional colocation, hyperscale and fiber infrastructure developer/operator · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** DC BLOX platform; financial ownership not resolved in this screen

Builds data centers and the connecting dark-fiber/subsea ecosystem. For this developer, the ability to join or extend its regional network is a commercial asset, not merely a generic dual-fiber checkbox.

**Geography:** Southeastern US; Atlanta; Birmingham; Carolinas; Tennessee; Florida; Indianapolis

**Scale:** Published hyperscale examples include 144 MW critical load on 68 Atlanta East acres, 200 MW critical load on 55 Atlanta West acres, and 40 MW Birmingham expansion. Cable-landing capacity has a different purpose from general AI IT capacity.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Atlanta West specifications include N+1, liquid-cooling provisions, two meet-me rooms and dark fiber to named Atlanta hubs and Myrtle Beach. [Source](https://www.dcblox.com/hyperscale-data-centers/)
- **published strategy / product:** Company expressly acquires land, secures power commitments and partners to develop hyperscale-ready sites with integrated dark fiber. [Source](https://www.dcblox.com/about/)
- **project evidence:** Myrtle Beach expansion used about 20 adjacent acres for additional cable systems and up to 20 MW, reflecting network-specific adjacency. [Source](https://www.dcblox.com/dc-blox-to-expand-myrtle-beach-cable-landing-station/)

**What changes the search**

- Own fiber and subsea gateway strategy creates identifiable corridor-specific preferences.
- Supports both regional edge and hyperscale; determine the actual product before screening acreage.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: a Southeast site that adds a useful node on the Atlanta–Myrtle Beach/Palm Coast network deserves priority over a geographically unrelated cheap parcel.
- Inference: require route economics, trench/ROW feasibility and named customer connectivity needs, rather than accepting a consumer broadband map.
- Inference: a 20–50 MW first phase can be plausible in a regional or staged campus; it does not by itself establish demand.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: parcel cannot cost-effectively connect to the relevant network/customer ecosystem.
- Inference: customer load is hyperscale training but the parcel only fits a small edge deployment, or vice versa.

**Questions to validate**

- Which fiber-route nodes currently lack enough usable data-center land?
- Do you need a regional 10–40 MW campus, a cable landing node, or a 100+ MW hyperscale position?

**Transaction route:** Land acquisition and partnership development are documented. Option assignment purchasing is unconfirmed.

**Acquisition signal:** Current corporate materials state an active land/power origination model and ongoing Southeast network expansion.

**Search brief:** Search industrial parcels and reuse opportunities near its regional fiber corridors and underserved population centers. Mooresville Business Park East is a Carolinas discovery lead; first verify useful connectivity to DC BLOX's network and whether Charlotte-area demand is open.

9 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified. 

**Sources**

- [Hyperscale campus specifications](https://www.dcblox.com/hyperscale-data-centers/) — DC BLOX; undated; checked 2026-10-29
- [Integrated land and fiber strategy](https://www.dcblox.com/about/) — DC BLOX; undated; checked 2026-10-29
- [Myrtle Beach expansion](https://www.dcblox.com/dc-blox-to-expand-myrtle-beach-cable-landing-station/) — DC BLOX; 2025-10-14; checked 2026-10-29

### 6. TECfusions

**Role:** adaptive-reuse high-density AI/HPC developer/operator · **Lane:** Industrial / facility reuse

**Parent / shared interests:** TECfusions platform

Adaptive industrial reuse with direct liquid cooling and staged grid-to-behind-the-meter energy strategies. Buyer fit depends on rapid practical reuse and funded customer deployment, not simply a large future MW headline.

**Geography:** Tucson; Pennsylvania/Pittsburgh; Virginia; San Antonio

**Scale:** January 2026 company report described 10 MW additions in Tucson and Pennsylvania; prior Tucson phase 14.4 MW. San Antonio flyer describes 111 acres and 20 MW initial/300 MW future. These are company marketing claims, not independently verified deliverability.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** January 2026 company report describes 20 MW of TensorWave expansion split between Tucson and Keystone, direct liquid cooling, and a Pennsylvania grid-to-BTM phasing strategy. [Source](https://tecfusions.com/category/new-kensington/)
- **project evidence:** San Antonio product flyer describes adaptive reuse and a target contract-to-deployment period below six months. [Source](https://tecfusions.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/San-Antonio-Texas.pdf)
- **project evidence:** October 2024 TensorWave announcement links customer capacity commitments to adaptive-reuse delivery across the portfolio. [Source](https://tecfusions.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/10/TECfusions-Secures-Landmark-1-GW-Capacity-Commitment-from-TensorWave-for-AI-Ready-Infrastructure.pdf)

**What changes the search**

- Adaptive reuse and direct-liquid-cooled AI are core to the model.
- Separate immediate deployment from aspirational gigawatt BTM growth.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: seek industrial buildings with reusable structure, electrical rooms and cooling space where permits/fit-out can actually be accelerated.
- Inference: on-site generation needs its own gas, air, noise and reliability proof; existing industrial zoning is not blanket authorization.
- Inference: a smaller near-term block can matter if it serves an identified cluster/customer and leaves room for expansion.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: retrofit cannot meet density, cooling or speed objectives.
- Inference: BTM plan substitutes speculative generation for a contracted service path.

**Questions to validate**

- Which industrial shell features let you deliver a 10–25 MW first phase within customer timing?
- Would you consider Mishawaka or other Midwest reuse, and what customer/energy prerequisites must be present?

**Transaction route:** Owns/develops reused campuses and leases AI infrastructure; option/PSA assignment policy unconfirmed.

**Acquisition signal:** Current company expansion report is a customer-specific signal. Its category webpage was searchable but returned 404 on direct opening, so use the underlying company PDFs and request current milestones before relying on dates.

**Search brief:** Screen former factories and industrial campuses, including the marketed AM General plant at Mishawaka. Require a structural/civil conversion test, contamination allocation, useful fiber and a written utility response; a building's prior peak usage does not prove today's capacity.

4 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

- [TensorWave commitment announcement](https://tecfusions.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/10/TECfusions-Secures-Landmark-1-GW-Capacity-Commitment-from-TensorWave-for-AI-Ready-Infrastructure.pdf) — TECfusions; 2024-10-31; checked 2026-10-29

### 7. Patmos

**Role:** adaptive-reuse AI/HPC developer/operator · **Lane:** Industrial / facility reuse

**Parent / shared interests:** Patmos Hosting Inc.

Converts existing industrial/urban infrastructure into AI/HPC capacity with rapid small initial blocks. This is a better conceptual buyer for a usable powered industrial building than for an undeveloped remote farm.

**Geography:** Kansas City; Dallas; Phoenix; Denver; verify current acquisition geographies

**Scale:** Kansas City example: initial 5 MW then another 7 MW; January 2026 financing announcement described a 35 MW upgrade program in a 421,112 SF former newspaper building on five acres. MW basis is not explicitly IT load.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** The company reported a $100M C-PACE financing for energy/HVAC/plumbing improvements at its Kansas City conversion, after staged initial deployments. [Source](https://www.patmos.tech/news/patmos-secures-100m-cpace)
- **project evidence:** Its Technology Campus model repurposes industrial infrastructure for high-density GPU/HPC while retaining coworking/events uses. [Source](https://www.patmos.tech/news/patmos-expands-kansas-city%E2%80%99s-ai-capability-at-new-datacenter-facility)
- **project evidence:** Current development-accounting recruitment confirms a portfolio of construction/development projects and owned data-center infrastructure. [Source](https://www.patmos.tech/careers/senior-accountant-data-center-development)

**What changes the search**

- Adaptive reuse and staged capacity are central to its demonstrated execution.
- Industrial shell, urban fiber and financing mechanics can be more useful than hundreds of acres.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: prioritize structurally usable shells with reusable electrical infrastructure and upgradeable cooling where a first phase can arrive quickly.
- Inference: distinguish genuinely reusable infrastructure from expensive demolition liabilities; industrial equipment nameplates do not establish import capacity.
- Inference: financing eligibility and retained ownership can matter to economics; do not assume a developer accepts a pure short-term ground lease.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: shell conversion takes longer or costs more than new construction.
- Inference: stranded industrial assets cannot be reused for high-density compute or customer geography is unsupported.

**Questions to validate**

- What building grids, floor loading, clearance and existing electrical conditions make a conversion worth underwriting?
- Would you enter a new Midwest market for a documented 10–30 MW initial phase, and can you take over an option?

**Transaction route:** Property acquisition/retrofit and long-term tenant leasing evidenced. Third-party option assignments and specific seller financing terms remain unverified.

**Acquisition signal:** January 2026 financing and current development staffing establish ongoing execution; no public open acquisition checklist found.

**Search brief:** Search former printing, manufacturing or enterprise-computing buildings in metros. The offered AM General plant in Mishawaka is a targeted reuse hypothesis: prove structural grid, contamination scope, cooling yards and carrier routes before claiming a fit.

2 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified. 

**Sources**

- [Kansas City C-PACE financing](https://www.patmos.tech/news/patmos-secures-100m-cpace) — Patmos; 2026-01-12; checked 2026-10-29
- [Technology Campus expansion](https://www.patmos.tech/news/patmos-expands-kansas-city%E2%80%99s-ai-capability-at-new-datacenter-facility) — Patmos; 2025-09-23; checked 2026-10-29
- [Active development accounting role](https://www.patmos.tech/careers/senior-accountant-data-center-development) — Patmos; undated; checked 2026-10-29

### 8. DartPoints

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** DartPoints; NOVA Infrastructure investment; legacy Immedion not a separate buyer

Regional colocation/interconnection developer pursuing enterprise, inference and larger AI workloads. Its recent Lexington brownfield acquisition is one of the clearest observed matches for a 20–30 MW first-phase strategy.

**Geography:** Kentucky; South Carolina; Indiana; Ohio; North Carolina; Louisiana

**Scale:** Lexington: approximately 29.5 acres, 343,000 square feet overall and 81,000 square feet of raised floor; planned 20–30 MW initial phase, up to 70 MW later. Capacity metric not consistently labeled IT in the release.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Lexington acquisition emphasizes an owned substation, existing data-center infrastructure and data-center zoning. [Source](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/dartpoints-acquires-lexington-kentucky-data-center-campus-to-support-ai-neo-cloud-hyperscale-and-enterprise-demand-302782916.html)
- **project evidence:** Planned Lexington design supports up to 200 kW per rack, 415V distribution, air and direct-to-chip cooling with low-water heat rejection. [Source](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/dartpoints-acquires-lexington-kentucky-data-center-campus-to-support-ai-neo-cloud-hyperscale-and-enterprise-demand-302782916.html)
- **project evidence:** 2025 expansion plan covered Greenville, Columbia, Columbus IN and Cincinnati with high-density capability and more than 25 MW additional utility capacity. [Source](https://dartpoints.com/press/dartpoints-puts-new-capital-to-work-launches-multi-market-expansion-to-power-hpc-ai-and-large-enterprise-growth/)

**What changes the search**

- Observed 20–30 MW initial phase is close to Syncranicity’s preferred lane.
- Hybrid air/liquid design supports mixed tenant needs.
- Local utility and economic-development engagement is part of acquisition rationale.

**Analyst inference**

- Prioritize pre-zoned brownfield sites with genuine reusable infrastructure and moderate expansion, rather than only huge training campuses.
- Regional network position between major peering markets can matter more than a simple straight-line distance to one metro.
- A new raw parcel must offer enough advantage to offset loss of existing building, zoning and substation readiness.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: no practical path from existing infrastructure to higher-density operation.
- Inference: isolated location lacks regional customer/interconnection demand.
- Inference: redevelopment or environmental costs remove the speed advantage.

**Questions to validate**

- Would another 20–30 MW first phase with 50–70 MW expansion fit the current mandate?
- How much of Lexington’s advantage came from zoning versus the owned substation and shell?
- Would you review an assignable option before title, utility and building-engineering diligence is complete?

**Transaction route:** Direct campus acquisition and phased redevelopment are evidenced; no published option-assignment or seller-financing policy.

**Acquisition signal:** Recent external brownfield acquisition gives a stronger current signal than a generic platform growth statement.

**Search brief:** Look across Southeast/Midwest secondary markets for former enterprise data centers or industrial shells with expandable substations, suitable floor loading, redundant carrier routes and a clear use approval. Screen for 20–30 MW initial service and expansion toward 50–70 MW as an analyst search hypothesis, not DartPoints’ published minimum.

6 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

- [Multi-market AI/HPC expansion](https://dartpoints.com/press/dartpoints-puts-new-capital-to-work-launches-multi-market-expansion-to-power-hpc-ai-and-large-enterprise-growth/) — DartPoints; 2025-08-21; checked 2026-10-29

### 9. Colovore

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Mid-scale / urban AI

**Parent / shared interests:** King Street-backed Colovore platform

Liquid-cooled metro-edge AI colocation specialist. This buyer’s geography is dictated by customer/network reach and low-latency use cases; proving MW at a distant rural site does not solve that requirement.

**Geography:** Silicon Valley; Reno/Sparks; Chicago; Austin/Hutto; Dallas

**Scale:** Austin/Hutto marketed at 40 MW in five 8 MW halls; Reno/Sparks facilities marketed at 16 MW each. Public pages do not clearly distinguish every total MW figure as facility versus IT.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Austin market page advertises liquid-cooled 5–600+ kW rack capability, PUE 1.3/WUE 0 and a less-than-5 ms metro-edge latency proposition. [Source](https://www.colovore.com/markets/austin)
- **project evidence:** Reno page positions facilities around Silicon Valley adjacency and low-latency access to West Coast users. [Source](https://www.colovore.com/markets/reno)
- **project evidence:** Company history describes King Street backing and committed Reno/Chicago capacity supporting expansion. [Source](https://www.colovore.com/company)

**What changes the search**

- High-density liquid cooling is the core product, not an optional add-on.
- 8 MW hall modules indicate a different scale rhythm from gigawatt campuses.
- Low-water operations and proximity to AI application users are part of market positioning.

**Analyst inference**

- A smaller metro-adjacent tract can be more valuable to this buyer than a huge isolated site.
- Latency claims must specify endpoints and measured route; the published marketing figure is not a guarantee from every parcel in Austin or Reno.
- Liquid cooling shifts the land screen toward electrical density, heat rejection, equipment yard, noise and mechanical-service access.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: site cannot meet tenant network/latency requirements.
- Inference: dense cooling/electrical equipment cannot fit under setbacks/noise limits.
- Inference: existing campus already meets market demand with better delivery timing.

**Questions to validate**

- Which markets and specific network endpoints are open for new site acquisition?
- What usable acreage and equipment-yard geometry does an 8 MW hall require?
- Would you buy raw land or only sites with a short permitted construction path?

**Transaction route:** Build-to-suit and owned/developed colocation facilities are evidenced. No public raw-land acquisition form or option assignment threshold found.

**Acquisition signal:** Current multi-market expansion gives a credible discovery target, but an announced campus may already satisfy local demand.

**Search brief:** Search industrial land or reusable shells in Hutto/Austin, Dallas, Reno/Sparks and other named markets with provable diverse fiber and relevant low-latency reach. Underwrite space for modular halls and cooling equipment. A 20–40 MW hypothesis is more appropriate than assuming the firm seeks hundreds of MW at any Texas location.

1 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

### 10. DataBank

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** DataBank platform

Enterprise colocation/interconnection platform also developing large AI campuses. A site must be assigned to the correct business lane rather than assuming every DataBank project is a 480 MW campus.

**Geography:** Dallas/Red Oak; Atlanta; Northern Virginia; Existing regional colocation markets

**Scale:** Red Oak: 292 acres and up to 480 MW critical IT at full build. Construction article states 240 MW IT in phase 1 from a 400 MW Oncor substation; another current campus paragraph says up to 300 MW. Resolve that conflict directly.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** The September 2024 acquisition announcement describes eight two-story buildings on 292 acres and a 480 MW campus. [Source](https://www.databank.com/resources/press-releases/databank-announces-development-of-480mw-data-center-campus-in-south-dallas/)
- **project evidence:** Construction detail describes 425,000 gross square feet per building, 200,000 square feet of data-center space and Q2 2026 initial ready-for-service target. [Source](https://www.databank.com/resources/blogs/building-the-future-inside-databanks-red-oak-campus-construction/)

**What changes the search**

- Dual regional-colocation and hyperscale growth lanes.
- Public capacity inconsistency illustrates why source reconciliation is a diligence deliverable.

**Analyst inference**

- Multi-story design can change land efficiency, but gross building square footage, data floor and IT MW must stay separate in parcel underwriting.
- For Dallas, compare any candidate against its controlled Red Oak expansion land; there must be a customer-location, power-date or cost advantage beyond proximity to other operators.
- Smaller regional opportunities should be pitched as enterprise/interconnection additions with local demand evidence, not extrapolated from the giant campus announcement.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: adequate MW but no advantage over existing expansion acreage.
- Inference: comparing utility substation capacity to IT load inflates the proposed customer product.

**Questions to validate**

- Which regional or hyperscale lane is currently acquiring sites?
- What is the actual initial/ultimate critical IT requirement and earliest useful service date?
- Would an external parcel complement or compete with Red Oak or another controlled campus?

**Transaction route:** Direct land purchases are documented. Option assignment appetite and willingness to reimburse third-party diligence are unverified.

**Acquisition signal:** The Red Oak acquisition confirms buying capability but is also existing competing supply. Announced initial service dates are not independent verification of energization.

**Search brief:** Search in-market expansion tracts or large campuses with a better phased utility date than existing inventory. Capture net usable land, multi-story feasibility and a side-by-side comparison with the nearest DataBank campus.

2 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

### 11. CyrusOne

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** CyrusOne

Wholesale and build-to-suit campuses, including reuse of former industrial/energy land where infrastructure and regional workforce can support phased development.

**Geography:** Texas; Northern Virginia; Phoenix; Chicago; North Carolina; Other existing markets

**Scale:** No universal acreage/MW minimum found. A Dallas-Allen facility sheet states 50 MW utility capacity; this is not IT load or an acquisition threshold. Sulphur Springs is still being evaluated as a multiphase campus.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** For Sulphur Springs, CyrusOne explicitly cites infrastructure access, available land, workforce proximity, reuse of a former lignite mine and natural visual buffers as reasons for selection. [Source](https://www.cyrusone.com/data-centers/north-america/sulphur-springs-texas)
- **project evidence:** The same project remains in diligence/planning; the company says final scale depends on engineering, environmental, utility, regulatory and community work. [Source](https://www.cyrusone.com/data-centers/north-america/sulphur-springs-texas)
- **project evidence:** Dallas-Allen DFW3 sheet lists 50 MW utility capacity and air-cooled closed-loop cooling without cooling towers. [Source](https://documents.cyrusone.com/wp-content/uploads/2023/06/2022-DFW3_Dallas-Allen.pdf)

**What changes the search**

- Explicit willingness to consider former mine land.
- Workforce and natural screening are documented selection reasons.

**Analyst inference**

- Former power, mining or industrial land can qualify despite a non-pristine history, but environmental liability, reclamation and geotechnical conditions must be priced and allocated. Prior disturbance is a lead, not a clearance.
- Mature vegetation and topography can have commercial value as visual/noise buffers if they do not compromise usable layout. Include a buffer concept rather than only gross acres.
- Low-water cooling means potable-water volume need not be the dominant screen; air-cooled heat rejection, summer performance, acoustics and the lawful initial fill remain relevant.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: legacy environmental/geotechnical liabilities cannot be allocated or remediated within schedule.
- Inference: community buffers disappear after net-buildable-acre analysis.

**Questions to validate**

- Would you buy brownfield land rights before remediation, and who would retain liabilities?
- Which workforce radius and air/noise envelope are required?
- Would staged power meet a current tenant need or merely add to the land bank?

**Transaction route:** Direct development is established; contract-assignment acceptance, liabilities assumed and required land-control milestone must be confirmed.

**Acquisition signal:** Sulphur Springs is an evaluated CyrusOne site, not an outside acquisition lead. It demonstrates a distinct brownfield thesis, not a general promise to buy disturbed land.

**Search brief:** Search large industrial or former-energy tracts near established workforce areas, with reusable infrastructure and defensible buffers. Ask for reclamation, title, subsurface and environmental records early.

1 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

### 12. Edged US

**Role:** waterless high-density AI/inference developer/operator · **Lane:** Mid-scale / urban AI

**Parent / shared interests:** Endeavour platform

Builds high-density facilities for regional inference and hyperscale customers using waterless cooling. Its public siting framework connects grid capacity, actual network pathways, transport, real-estate conditions and community alignment from the outset.

**Geography:** Atlanta; Chicago; Columbus; Dallas; Des Moines; Kansas City; Phoenix; Omaha/Council Bluffs

**Scale:** Observed US campus sizes include 13 MW Des Moines, 24 MW Dallas/Columbus, 26 MW Kansas City, 36 MW Phoenix and 169 MW Atlanta. Current examples demonstrate more than a single hyperscale minimum.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** Published site-selection process evaluates scalable power, fiber pathways, transportation, real-estate conditions, community alignment, permitting, constructability and future scale. [Source](https://edged.us/news/edged-accelerates-ai-infrastructure-deployment-through-data-driven-site-selection)
- **project evidence:** Atlanta third building is 42 MW critical capacity and designed for 400+ kW/rack liquid or 120+ kW/rack air cooling; this is facility design, not a site minimum. [Source](https://edged.us/news/edged-us-tops-out-ultra---efficient-42-mw-data-center-optimized-for-ai-inference-at-scale-at-its-atlanta-campus)
- **project evidence:** Atlanta campus specifications include two meet-me rooms, multiple providers and four diverse entrances, with waterless cooling. [Source](https://edged.us/atlanta)
- **project evidence:** September 2026 release reports topping out two buildings of its 200 MW Council Bluffs campus; this is already controlled competing inventory, not land available for origination. [Source](https://edged.us/news/edged-us-celebrates-topping-out-of-oma01-1-and-oma01-2-in-council-bluffs)

**What changes the search**

- Published geospatial site-selection workflow is unusually explicit.
- Waterless heat rejection changes the land/cooling screen, while extreme rack density raises equipment-yard and electrical design demands.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: a near-metro 20–40 MW site can be a more relevant first conversation than a remote gigawatt promise.
- Inference: carrier routes serving regional users and a practical dry-cooling equipment layout are key differentiators.
- Inference: the same city is not enough; compare the new parcel against its owned/under-construction campus and prove incremental customer need.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: regional latency/customer thesis is absent.
- Inference: claimed zero cooling-water use is incorrectly taken to mean no fire/domestic water or no heat/noise impacts.

**Questions to validate**

- Which metros require land beyond your current campus pipeline?
- What first-phase critical MW, density, network endpoints and expansion margin should a new parcel be designed around?

**Transaction route:** Develops and operates facilities, including reused industrial land; landowner/JV/option acquisition route not publicly specified.

**Acquisition signal:** 2026 Atlanta construction and current national portfolio establish activity. Existing facilities and pipeline are comparables, not available land for Syncranicity to option.

**Search brief:** Search industrially compatible metropolitan sites with 20–50 MW staged expansion, network routes and cooling yards. Council Bluffs 121-acre broker listing is a regional-match lead; flood exposure near Lake Manawa and local fiber/service details remain gating unknowns.

3 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

### 13. PowerHouse Data Centers

**Role:** hyperscale powered-shell and turnkey developer · **Lane:** Land / campus infrastructure

**Parent / shared interests:** Division of American Real Estate Partners (AREP); project JVs include Harrison Street and Chirisa

Real-estate-led campus development, using both powered shells and turnkey assets and partnering with land/capital owners. Distinct partner projects must not be counted as independent PowerHouse and Chirisa takeouts for the same parcel.

**Geography:** Northern Virginia; Richmond/Chesterfield; Dallas–Fort Worth; Reno; Charlotte; Pennsylvania; Kentucky

**Scale:** Irving example: 50 acres and 200 MW marketed campus capacity; Reno: 49 acres and 200 MW. Project MW basis should be clarified; these are not published minimums.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Irving acquisition placed a 200 MW, three-building powered-shell campus on 50 acres in a major connectivity hub, with adjacent Oncor substation access. [Source](https://www.powerhousedata.com/news/powerhouse-expands-into-texas-plans-200mw-campus-in-dallas)
- **project evidence:** PowerHouse and Chirisa acquired 104 acres adjacent to an existing campus for AI-ready phased development; it offers powered-shell, build-to-suit and turnkey products. [Source](https://www.powerhousedata.com/news/chirisa-powerhouse-launching-hyperscale-campus-in-chesterfield)
- **project evidence:** Company press archive lists 2025 Carlisle 1.35 GW JV and January 2026 Arcola hyperscale lease announcements. [Source](https://www.powerhousedata.com/press-releases)

- Inference: urban/near-market land can work at relatively compact acreage when multistory design and permanent power support high density.
- Inference: sellers offering a credible JV or assembled adjacent land may be more useful than a thin option with no engineering access.
- Inference: first-tenant underwriting and market connectivity remain decisive even if supply capacity is confirmed.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: no hyperscale tenant demand at the proposed node.
- Inference: power dates or capital obligations prevent a marketable powered-shell schedule.

**Questions to validate**

- Would a new parcel supplement your existing regional campus or compete with capacity you already own?
- At what diligence stage would you acquire a third-party contract, and who carries utility deposits?

**Transaction route:** Documented fee-simple land buys and project JVs. No public commitment to option assignments; validate whether it pays for origination or wants a direct seller contract.

**Acquisition signal:** Continuing project/JV activity is evidenced. Individual historical delivery dates must be refreshed and are not guarantees.

**Search brief:** Search 40–150+ acre industrial/technology parcels in active metros with substation room and high density building feasibility. Reno Electric Avenue is a targeted example, conditional on live utility service economics and avoiding overlap with its already controlled Reno campus.

### 14. Chirisa Technology Parks

**Role:** wholesale, hyperscale and AI developer/operator · **Lane:** Industrial / facility reuse

Combines brownfield reuse, greenfield extensions and powered-shell/turnkey delivery in established growth markets. It explicitly allows purchase or joint venture for customer build-to-suit work, an unusually useful route for a land originator to validate.

**Scale:** Observed portfolio includes 13 MW utility power reuse in Lynnwood, 28 MW Chicago buildings and a 230-acre Richmond campus. Large project figures are utility power where the company says so; do not equate them to critical IT load.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Current portfolio includes adaptive reuse at Piscataway and Lynnwood alongside a large Virginia greenfield pipeline. [Source](https://chirisatechnologyparks.com/)
- **project evidence:** PowerHouse/Chirisa acquired adjacent 104-acre Meadowville land for phased AI-ready facilities; existing campus adjacency was central. - Inference: land adjacency to existing network/energy infrastructure is a better screening input than acreage alone.
- Inference: its established land bank can displace a new raw site unless the new site wins on cost, latency or timing.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: retrofit cannot deliver materially faster or cheaper than an existing reserved campus phase.
- Inference: new shell cannot support desired high density, redundancy or customer architecture.

**Questions to validate**

- Which markets still need new land or brownfield capacity beyond current reserved phases?
- What diligence package makes a landowner/JV opportunity actionable, and can the seller's option be assigned?

**Transaction route:** Powered shell, turnkey, build-to-suit and JV/purchase. Assignment or fee terms require specific confirmation.

**Acquisition signal:** 2025 Blue Owl JV announcements and current reserved campus phases show funded growth, while some existing pipeline already has users.

**Search brief:** Search connected conversion buildings or adjacent expansion tracts in its five disclosed US clusters. NG Data Center in Chester is a targeted reuse/market-adjacency hypothesis; test actual shell suitability and whether existing commitments leave incremental demand.

### 15. 365 Data Centers

**Role:** Operator/development partner; adaptive-reuse emphasis · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** 365 platform; 2026 development partnership with Aphorio Carter

A 2026 partnership with Aphorio Carter targets identifying, converting and developing AI-ready facilities; 365 would operate them long-term while its partner contributes real-estate/development capabilities.

**Geography:** Aurora CO; Simpsonville KY; Trumbull CT; Louisville KY; Harrisonburg VA; Columbus OH

**Scale:** Approximately 200 MW aggregate initial pipeline, six sites; 50 to 200+ kW per cabinet; 9–24 month indicated delivery after May 2026 announcement. Pipeline MW basis not identified; not a per-site minimum.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** May 2026 release identifies Aurora and Simpsonville LOIs and additional planned LOIs in Trumbull, Louisville, Harrisonburg and Columbus. [Source](https://365datacenters.com/365-data-centers-and-aphorio-carter-launch-200-mw-ai-ready-data-center-pipeline/)
- **project evidence:** Facilities are designed for liquid-to-chip cooling at 50–200+ kW/cabinet; availability is subject to final contracts/internal approvals. [Source](https://365datacenters.com/365-data-centers-and-aphorio-carter-launch-200-mw-ai-ready-data-center-pipeline/)

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: Greenfield schedule cannot meet conversion-led deployment window.
- Inference: Only nameplate power; no budget for higher-density cooling and electrical distribution.

- Which of the six markets still need uncommitted sites?
- Who buys the land—365 or Aphorio Carter—and what should an assignment package contain?
- Is a powered shell sufficient, or must existing commissioned plant be reusable?

**Search brief:** Source powered buildings or rapid-conversion industrial sites in six named markets, documenting reusable plant, liquid-cooling retrofit costs and feasible 50–200+ kW racks.

### 16. Ada Infrastructure

**Role:** Developer / operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

Global developer explicitly targeting strategic sites in major North American hubs. Virginia’s large planned campus provides unusually detailed layout, electrical, fiber and cooling evidence. Those specifications are a template for qualification questions, not a claim that every future acquisition must reproduce the same campus.

**What changes the search**

- Explicit separation of IT capacity, MVA and voltage allows accurate comparison to utility service proposals.

- Inference: nominal diverse connectivity collapses to a common physical route.

**Questions to validate**

- Which North American metros beyond Virginia are currently approved for acquisition?
- What is the smallest independently acceptable IT phase and ultimate campus requirement?
- At which point will Ada acquire assignable land/control rights and rely on third-party studies?

**Transaction route:** Institutional site acquisition/development and long-term customer delivery. Public option-assignment or landowner-JV policy unverified.

**Acquisition signal:** Current US page explicitly seeks major-hub expansion beyond Virginia; actual target metros and acquisition windows remain undisclosed.

**Search brief:** Search major-hub industrial corridors with a credible large phased campus, redundant electrical paths, a utility-owned substation strategy, constructible diverse fiber and entitlement readiness. Treat a 20–50 MW-only site as an exception to qualify first.

### 17. AirTrunk

**Role:** Developer / operator · **Lane:** International watch

**Parent / shared interests:** Blackstone and CPP Investments backed; do not conflate with other Blackstone portfolio developers

Large hyperscale platform oriented to Asia Pacific and the Middle East. Recent projects use contracted anchors, adjacent campus expansion and large-scale financing. It is a useful benchmark for what a bankable campus looks like, but US financial sponsors do not create evidence of US site demand.

**Scale:** Johor JHB1: 150+ MW contracted campus; adjacent JHB2: 270+ MW greenfield development. These are project figures, not a published minimum and not explicitly IT load in the cited IFC summary.

- Would a small phased parcel be considered or is adjacent-campus expansion preferred?

- **project evidence:** August 2026 Tydal lease covers four halls over two delivery phases with tenant credit-support conditions. [Source](https://bitdeer.gcs-web.com/news-releases/news-release-details/bitdeer-announces-47-billion-16-year-aihpc-data-center-lease)
- **project evidence:** January operations report identifies Tennessee/Washington conversion plans and Ohio HPC/AI projects separately from mining facilities. [Source](https://ir.bitdeer.com/news-releases/news-release-details/bitdeer-announces-january-2026-production-and-operations-update)
- **project evidence:** Clarington power timing was expressly exposed to neighboring-party litigation in the January update. [Source](https://ir.bitdeer.com/news-releases/news-release-details/bitdeer-announces-january-2026-production-and-operations-update)

**What changes the search**

- Public operations tables distinguish facility type rather than equating all MW with AI.
- Small existing conversions coexist with very large new projects.
- Litigation, access and third-party rights can dominate the power timetable.

**Analyst inference**

- Existing electrical assets and adaptable structures can shorten delivery, but permits and neighboring land rights must be examined early.
- A compact conversion may fit a specific deployment, while a new raw-land campus probably needs much greater scale.
- Power price cannot compensate for financing or tenant-credit conditions that block construction.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: litigation or third-party rights makes delivery uncertain.
- Inference: shell cannot accommodate AI cooling or floor/electrical demands.
- Inference: tenant credit support fails financing requirements.

**Questions to validate**

- What US new-site minimum differs from conversion minimums?
- Would you accept a site with utility service but no AI building permits?
- What credit and financing milestone must precede a land purchase?

**Transaction route:** Owned campus development with long-term colocation/services leases and conditional institutional credit support. No published option-assignment intake policy located.

**Acquisition signal:** Actual AI lease demonstrates capability; US pipeline maturity and legal status require project-level confirmation.

**Search brief:** Seek existing industrial/data-center facilities in Ohio, Tennessee or the Pacific Northwest with a feasible AI retrofit, or larger power positions with clean land rights. Require a utility agreement and legal schedule assessment. A small powered building is a more defensible test than assuming a new 20 MW rural greenfield campus matches Tydal economics.

1 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

### 28. Bluebird Fiber (formerly Bluebird Network)

**Role:** Fiber-network operator and regional colocation facility developer · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** Bluebird Fiber; do not conflate with similarly named Denver site-originator

Operates regional data centers whose value is tied to Bluebird fiber, peering and resilience. Its underground Springfield location is a special-purpose asset, not evidence that ordinary farmland is interchangeable with its campuses.

**Geography:** Springfield MO; Bettendorf / Quad Cities IA-IL; Midwest fiber footprint

**Scale:** Published offerings include cabinet/cage/room colocation and 8,000–10,000 square feet available whitespace. No current published greenfield acreage/MW acquisition threshold established.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Current data-center page identifies Springfield Underground and Quad Cities sites, with multiple utility feeds, backup systems and network services. [Source](https://bluebirdfiber.com/data-centers/)
- **project evidence:** Published Springfield latency examples are 5–8 ms to Kansas City, 8–10 ms to St Louis and 10–12 ms to Chicago; these are service examples, not universal site-selection limits. [Source](https://bluebirdfiber.com/data-centers/)
- **project evidence:** October 2026 release describes a multi-year optical-network upgrade supporting 100–400G services and diverse Midwest routes. [Source](https://bluebirdfiber.com/ekinops-powers-bluebird-fibers-midwest-network-investments-for-growing-infrastructure-demand/)

**What changes the search**

- Regional low-latency network and disaster-resilience differentiation.
- Underground limestone facility is an unusual existing-site feature, not a general construction requirement.

**Analyst inference**

- Sites should improve a known route, peering location or customer catchment; this buyer is not automatically a taker for 20–50 MW rural land.
- A proposal for resilience/disaster recovery needs both geographic separation and customer-usable network latency.
- The latest investment signal concerns fiber upgrades, so any new real-estate acquisition appetite needs direct confirmation.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: No network/customer synergy.
- Inference: Rural facility cannot offer credible geographic or latency benefit.
- Inference: Large speculative campus exceeds evidenced expansion strategy.

**Questions to validate**

- Is Bluebird actively acquiring/developing additional colocation real estate?
- Which fiber nodes or customer areas need capacity?
- Would a partner own the site while Bluebird supplies network and operations?

**Transaction route:** Own/operate regional centers; current public land-option acquisition or national development mandate not found.

**Acquisition signal:** 2026 network investment is verified; no new campus land solicitation was located.

**Search brief:** Search existing regional carrier or enterprise facilities on Bluebird routes with customer demand, redundant power and a clear resilient-network role. Treat as secondary discovery, not a first wave raw-land buyer.

**Parent / shared interests:** TPG Real Estate manages Frederick project; Catellus master-development platform

Master-plans, remediates and installs shared infrastructure, then sells parcels to building developers. Included as an active land-development platform, not as a direct colocation operator. Quantum Loophole has been replaced in this roster because Catellus now handles Frederick development.

**Geography:** Frederick County, Maryland; US redevelopment markets; new data-center acquisition mandate unverified

**Scale:** Frederick example: approximately 2,100 acres, 2.4 GW under development and 17.4M SF entitled according to Catellus. These are project claims, not delivered capacity or a general minimum.

- **project evidence:** December 2024 statement names TPG as manager and Catellus sole developer; Quantum Loophole has no active development role there. [Source](https://www.catellus.com/updates/statement-from-tpg-real-estate-and-catellus-development-corporation-on-the-quantum-frederick-project)

- Horizontal/master development rather than ownership of every data-center building.
- Official county decision conflicts with the promotional impression that the proposed community agreement is already secured; use the county action for approval status.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: complex former industrial land with recoverable infrastructure, subdivision potential and regional interconnection advantages is more relevant than a small standalone rural tract.
- Inference: public partnership, remediation allocation and long entitlement runway are unusually important for its master-development model.
- Inference: no current evidence establishes that Catellus is seeking a second new data-center campus; validate mandate before prospect spending.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: no scalable backbone infrastructure or untenable remediation liabilities.
- Current jurisdiction gate: unresolved Frederick application restrictions require parcel-specific review.

**Questions to validate**

- Are you seeking new data-center master-plan sites outside Frederick, and in what markets?
- Would the platform acquire a controlled brownfield position or act only as development partner for an institutional owner?

**Transaction route:** Land master-development and parcel disposition evidenced. Willingness to buy options on new sites unknown.

**Acquisition signal:** Active Frederick delivery confirmed; new-site procurement not confirmed.

**Search brief:** Screen large brownfield assemblages near existing demand hubs only after Catellus confirms a new-site acquisition brief. Do not market new Frederick acreage as approved or unaffected by the current pause.

### 30. Cipher Digital

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Energy-led AI

**Parent / shared interests:** Cipher Digital Inc., formerly Cipher Mining Inc. (effective February 20, 2026)

Industrial-scale HPC developer that deliberately secures new site options, leases capacity to major tenants and finances construction at project level. This is unusually direct public evidence of option-based site control, although it does not validate any proposed assignment fee.

**Geography:** Texas; Ohio

**Scale:** Apollo option: approximately 288 acres, up to 900 MW near San Antonio. Earlier contracted HPC projects totaled 600 MW gross across two leases; no universal minimum-size requirement published.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** August 2026 update says Cipher acquired an option over Apollo within 25 miles of San Antonio; the site was submitted as a studied load in ERCOT Batch Zero. [Source](https://investors.cipherdigital.com/news-releases/news-release-details/cipher-digital-provides-second-quarter-2026-business-update)
- **project evidence:** The same release specifically highlights flat buildable terrain and proximity supporting fiber connectivity. [Source](https://investors.cipherdigital.com/news-releases/news-release-details/cipher-digital-provides-second-quarter-2026-business-update)
- **project evidence:** February 2026 update documents the Cipher Digital rename and pivot toward HPC, with project funding tied to major leases. [Source](https://investors.cipherdigital.com/news-releases/news-release-details/cipher-digital-provides-fourth-quarter-and-full-year-2025/)

- Demonstrated use of land options.
- Flat terrain and fiber-market adjacency explicitly explained in a new-site announcement.

- An option with inexpensive land but no progressed load study will compare poorly to Apollo’s documented development position.
- Metro-adjacent network reach can improve an energy-rich site’s value; it is not equivalent to a city-center edge latency promise.
- A new small parcel needs either expansion acreage or a reason a specific tenant cannot be served elsewhere in Cipher’s portfolio.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: project size too small relative to institutional execution costs.
- Inference: transmission proximity substituted for utility process progress.

**Questions to validate**

- Do you accept assignment from third-party originators, and what scope of exclusivity is required?
- Is a 20–50 MW phase useful if expansion beyond that is limited?

**Transaction route:** Option acquisition is evidenced; terms, seller, assignment origin and consideration are not disclosed in the cited release. Do not equate acquiring an option with buying Bill’s existing option.

**Acquisition signal:** Direct current new-site acquisition evidence, stronger than merely an old facility expansion announcement.

**Search brief:** Look for Texas or other proven target-market sites with a substantial buildable footprint, expandable utility path, demonstrable fiber access and assignable study/control rights. Ask whether Cipher currently wants options before studies, after studies, or only after customer screening. A marketable packet needs a defined first phase and the utility evidence supporting the larger campus.

### 31. CleanArc Data Centers

**Role:** hyperscale developer and development/leasing partner · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** CleanArc platform; Crow Holdings land partnership in Dallas

Its published site strategy is Tier 1 Adjacent: achieve hub-quality connectivity without every core-market constraint. This is an unusually specific filter that should be tested using network engineering rather than map distance.

**Geography:** Caroline County, Virginia; Dallas; Austin; Chicago planned growth; Atlanta planned growth

**Scale:** Dallas example: 40 acres, 245 MW marketed ultimate campus, 70 MW first building planned late 2027. Figure basis should be clarified before treating it as critical IT load.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** CleanArc defines target Tier 1 Adjacent locations as typically within approximately two milliseconds round-trip latency of a major carrier hotel or internet exchange. [Source](https://www.cleanarcdatacenters.com/locations/)
- **project evidence:** Dallas project uses Crow Holdings-owned urban infill land near Infomart/2323 Bryan, with CleanArc leading technical design, substation construction and tenant engagement. [Source](https://www.cleanarcdatacenters.com/press-release/cleanarc-data-centers-selected-to-lead-development-and-leasing-for-crow-holdings-245mw-flagship-campus-in-central-dallas-2/)

**What changes the search**

- Explicit ~2 ms RTT target goes beyond a generic fiber requirement.
- Development-and-leasing partnership on another owner's land is documented.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: route length, optical equipment and actual RTT to the named hub should be a pre-screen gate; straight-line miles are insufficient.
- Inference: a landowner partnership can be viable without the developer initially buying fee title.
- Inference: compact, highly connected sites may compete well with large campuses when density and power support the use.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Published strategy mismatch: cannot plausibly meet the relevant hub RTT.
- Inference: property is cheap because fiber build and route rights consume the schedule/cost advantage.

**Questions to validate**

- Which carrier hotel or IX and what measured RTT define the requirement for the next site?
- Would you partner with a landowner through an assignable option, and what power milestone must be reached first?

**Transaction route:** Landowner/developer partnership evidenced. Option assignment, ground lease and fee schedule are transaction-specific and unverified.

**Acquisition signal:** February 2026 central-Dallas appointment and published future-growth markets are useful current search signals, not an acquisition guarantee.

**Search brief:** Start from Infomart, 2323 Bryan, Atlanta interconnection hubs or the relevant Virginia hub and trace feasible low-latency routes outward. Screen land only inside a defensible network envelope, then test scalable service and land-control economics.

### 32. CloudHQ

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** CloudHQ

Large customer-configurable campuses tied to major network ecosystems. Its Culpeper example uses proximity to existing data centers and fiber as the bridge from a secondary location to Northern Virginia demand.

**Geography:** Northern Virginia; Culpeper; Santa Clara; Other disclosed global campuses

**Scale:** Culpeper official page is inconsistent: headline says 215 MW critical IT, while its narrative says up to 142 MW in three buildings. Both are recorded; confirm current approved program directly.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** CloudHQ explains Culpeper selection through nearby existing data centers, including an Equinix business exchange, multiple high-density fiber providers and an on-site substation. [Source](https://cloudhq.com/campus/clp-campus/)

**What changes the search**

- Tenant-specific building/critical-load customization.
- Culpeper network adjacency illustrates location logic; capacity discrepancy remains unresolved.

**Analyst inference**

- A network-adjacent secondary market is more defensible than an isolated power-only tract; map the specific hub and available fiber routes rather than broadly describing the state as a data-center market.
- Prepare a flexible campus concept with independently usable building phases; the final tenant may alter critical-load and building size requirements.

- Inference: no useful fiber connection to the target metro/customer ecosystem.
- Inference: parcel configuration allows only a rigid layout inconsistent with tenant customization.

- Which customer and route-latency constraints define the acceptable geography?
- Will CloudHQ acquire transferable site rights before the tenant-specific design is final?

**Transaction route:** Developer/operator campus model is clear. An option purchaser, assignment fee and acquisition evidence threshold are not publicly verified.

**Acquisition signal:** The Culpeper campus is an existing CloudHQ project with a published 2027 target. It confirms a product strategy, not a live request for another nearby site.

**Search brief:** Search large, flexible sites in established or network-adjacent markets where on-site substations and distinct fiber connections can be built. Model multiple customer layouts instead of fixing an unsupported universal MW/acre ratio.

**Role:** Interconnection and hyperscale-edge developer/operator · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** Private developer platform; parent ownership not reverified in this pass

Builds network-neutral interconnection hubs and larger Scalelogix capacity. For land sourcing, metro network gravity and proximity to existing customers can outweigh cheap rural land.

**Geography:** North America; Ashburn VA; Columbus OH; Denver CO; Canadian metros

**Scale:** February 2026 Ashburn acquisition: 38 acres; 85 MW initial power and more than 300 MW potential over time; preliminary planning around 2030. These are campus plans, not company-wide minimums or current delivered MW.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Cologix acquired 38 acres on Beaumeade Circle in Ashburn, describing industrial zoning, dense fiber and proximity to existing digital infrastructure. [Source](https://cologix.com/news/cologix-expands-ashburn-presence-with-strategic-land-acquisition-supporting-5b-long-term-northern-virginia-growth-plan/)
- **project evidence:** The announced program serves hyperscale, AI-ready, enterprise, carrier and locally based demand and emphasizes interconnection and hybrid-cloud architectures. - The 2030 planning horizon shows willingness to land-bank strategically, but should not be generalized to a founder’s short option term.
- A suburban Ohio or Denver candidate needs two real fiber routes and a link to Cologix customer ecosystems.

- Inference: Low-cost land is outside the necessary network/enterprise market.
- Inference: Fiber is technically nearby but diverse paths or cloud onramps cannot be constructed.
- Inference: Option term expires years before the buyer’s land-bank development decision.

**Questions to validate**

- Which Scalelogix/interconnection markets have an unmet land requirement?
- What dark-fiber routes, round-trip latency and carrier access must a new parcel support?
- Is strategic land banking or near-term ready-for-service the current priority?

**Transaction route:** Direct land acquisition for owned campus development demonstrated; acceptance of option assignments not disclosed.

**Acquisition signal:** February 2026 Ashburn acquisition is strong land-buying evidence but adds already controlled competing supply.

**Search brief:** Search industrially entitled metro parcels near major carrier interconnection with a staged utility plan; do not use the Ashburn 85 MW as a universal intake threshold.

Long-lived, single-tenant hyperscale campuses with repeatable modular construction. Multi-building economics, site-preparation support and long-term utility/local-government coordination are central to its development examples.

**Acquisition signal:** Meridian confirms willingness to enter a secondary Southeastern market when infrastructure and public partners align. It does not show an active search for any inexpensive Southeastern tract.
