**Scale:** Denver expansion: 15 acres, three buildings and 60 critical MW; DE3 initial building 18 MW compute on three floors. This is an unusually concrete infill precedent, not a company-wide minimum.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** DE3 is planned to connect by high-count dark fiber to CoreSite DE1/DE2 interconnection hubs. [Source](https://www.coresite.com/data-center/de3-denver-co)
- **project evidence:** CoreSite’s Denver community page describes multi-tenant facilities serving technology, telecom, education, government, commerce and healthcare, and an Xcel-powered 18 MW DE3. [Source](https://www.coresite.com/en-us/data-centers/denver/de3-construction)

**What changes the search**

- High-count dark fiber to existing hubs is central to the actual expansion design.
- Urban multi-tenant economics differ from single-tenant AI megacampuses.

**Analyst inference**

- Search metropolitan industrial infill with feasible fiber construction to an existing interconnection node before searching cheap rural land. Location is part of the service sold.
- Multi-story development can create meaningful capacity on a modest site, but zoning height, loading, fire access and equipment yards may be decisive.
- Community agreements and construction impacts can be material even at 15 acres; a technically feasible urban site still needs a workable neighborhood path.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: remote power-rich tract cannot deliver the required interconnection ecosystem.
- Inference: urban setbacks, height or neighborhood conditions prevent the intended density.

**Questions to validate**

- Which existing hub would a new site have to connect to?
- What route length, strand count and network diversity are required?
- Would you acquire an entitled infill option before final building permits?

**Transaction route:** Development/acquisition capability is evident; no public option-assignment purchase policy found. Route opportunities to real estate/development with an explicit network-extension case.

**Acquisition signal:** DE3 is an existing controlled project, with late-2026 availability described by current company materials. It is not a purchasable outside lead.

**Search brief:** Search infill parcels and redevelopment buildings in its existing metro markets that can physically and economically connect to carrier-hotel assets. Specify customer catchment, fiber routes and multi-story net capacity.

1 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

Enterprise colocation and interconnection platform whose published growth strategy emphasizes capacity inside its existing estate. This makes it an important negative control for buyer-first sourcing: active data-center growth does not necessarily mean demand for raw greenfield land.

**Scale:** Public materials describe 80 data centers on 64 sites in 21 metros and 500+ MW of power. Prospectus identifies up to approximately 670 MW potential internal expansion; this is not all approved new IT capacity.

- Inference: new build economics exceed internal expansion alternatives.

- Which metros lack internal expansion capacity?
- What acquisition return and time-to-bill thresholds apply to a third-party shell or adjacent tract?

**Transaction route:** Facility acquisition, leasing and internal development are evidenced; no general raw-land or option-assignment mandate found.

**Acquisition signal:** Strong capacity demand but low-confidence raw-land buyer fit unless a specific metro gap or adjacency need is established.

**Search brief:** Search within established Csquare metros for reusable data-center shells or adjacent expansion tracts with network ecosystems and existing customer demand. ### 46. EdgeCore Digital Infrastructure

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** EdgeCore platform

Single-tenant hyperscale campuses at scale, including markets outside the tight core of Northern Virginia that retain access to its network. The target is an expansion-ready product with substantial early power and permit work completed.

**Geography:** Louisa County; Culpeper; Northern Virginia; Phoenix; Reno; Silicon Valley

**Scale:** Louisa current specifications: 697 acres, at least six buildings, over 1.1 GW critical IT and 3.1 million square feet. Older June 2025 announcement says 3.9 million square feet; retain version/date rather than merge.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** EdgeCore explains Louisa selection as scalable land and gigawatt-scale power within network reach of Ashburn. [Source](https://edgecore.com/locations/louisa-county-data-center)
- **project evidence:** It describes single-tenant facilities with flexible air/direct-to-chip cooling and WUE below 0.01 L/kWh for that campus. [Source](https://edgecore.com/locations/louisa-county-data-center)
- **project evidence:** June 2025 acquisition announcement confirms purchase of 697 acres and collaboration with Rappahannock Electric Cooperative/Hyperscale Energy. [Source](https://edgecore.com/resources/press-releases/new-louisa-county-virginia-data-center-campus)

**What changes the search**

- Single hyperscale tenant per building.
- Explicit network-adjacent secondary-location strategy.

**Analyst inference**

- Search within credible fiber reach of major cloud hubs where land and delivery capacity improve on the congested core. A route-engineered latency map is more useful than road distance to Ashburn.
- Usable acres must support multiple independent tenant buildings, dedicated infrastructure and later expansion. A narrow 100-acre strip may be much less useful than a lower gross-acre total with clean geometry.
- A small first project should proceed only if EdgeCore confirms an exception or expansion role; the current evidence predominantly supports very large campus requirements.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: cheap power far outside the tenant’s network envelope.
- Inference: sufficient first-phase land but no economical multi-building expansion path.

**Questions to validate**

- What maximum routed latency to Ashburn or another hub is acceptable?
- What minimum first tenant block and reserved expansion land are required?
- At which power/permit milestone will you pay for controlled land rights?

**Transaction route:** External land purchase is confirmed. No public acceptance criteria for third-party option assignments or originator compensation were found.

**Acquisition signal:** Louisa demonstrates the buying model but is already acquired. Its quoted capacity is a campus plan, not transferable grid capacity for other land nearby.

**Search brief:** Search large contiguous tracts near established fiber corridors with a phased power and entitlement advantage; map the exact latency/route relationship to the target hyperscaler hub.

[Source](https://ce.goodman.com/about-goodman/media-centre/news/2026/databank-and-goodman-group-partner-to-open-new-landmark-data-center-in-los-angeles)
- **project evidence:** October 2025 acquisition of 45.8-acre San Jose infill site supports data-center redevelopment alongside warehouse/manufacturing uses. [Source](https://www.goodman.com/investor-centre/announcements-media/2025/goodman-acquires-prime-silicon-valley-site-for-data-center-redevelopment)

**What changes the search**

- A land developer can supply the site and development capability while a partner such as DataBank operates the capacity.
- Silicon Valley and Vernon comparables demonstrate infill redevelopment; their prices/capacities are not universal acquisition thresholds.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: favor urban industrial redevelopment with a credible utility schedule, entitlements and a feasible high-density building envelope, rather than selecting primarily on dollars per acre.
- Inference: a parcel that preserves industrial rental income during planning could fit the mixed digital/industrial strategy, but environmental liabilities and tenant termination costs need quantification.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: remote land with no metro-latency/customer advantage.
- Inference: inability to clear existing uses or demolish/redevelop economically.

**Questions to validate**

- Which US metros are open for new land acquisition outside the existing JV pipeline?
- Would Goodman purchase controlled rights before full entitlements, and what are the minimum documented power and planning milestones?
- Is a compact urban parcel acceptable independently or only as an assemblage?

**Transaction route:** Demonstrated direct land acquisition and operator JV. Acceptance of an assignable option from an independent originator is unverified.

**Acquisition signal:** US JV explicitly seeks additional capacity-constrained markets; this is a stronger acquisition signal than a generic global footprint.

**Search brief:** Search industrial infill in Los Angeles/Vernon and Silicon Valley with expandable rights, actual power schedule, network route evidence and an interim-use cash-flow story. Do not substitute Vernon, Texas for Vernon, California.

### 56. Google

**Role:** Self-build hyperscaler and third-party development customer · **Lane:** Owner-builders

**Parent / shared interests:** Alphabet

Large cloud/AI buildout with a particularly relevant power-first Wilbarger County precedent: Google accepts an integrated construction-ready land, interconnection and new generation package, rather than evidence of line proximity alone.

**Geography:** United States; Wilbarger County TX; Texas; Ohio; Iowa; Nebraska; Oklahoma

**Scale:** No standard minimum campus acreage/MW published in reviewed sources. Google’s 7,800 MW Texas generation/capacity contracting figure is energy portfolio capacity, NOT the size or load of Wilbarger or Hinds 110.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Google announced a Wilbarger County data center under construction on February 24, 2026, using advanced air cooling and co-located AES clean power. [Source](https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/infrastructure-and-cloud/global-network/data-center-wilbarger-county/)
- **project evidence:** AES described construction-ready land and interconnection agreements, generation under 20-year PPAs, and long-term energy-management services for the Wilbarger campus. [Source](https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-uniblog-publish-prod/documents/wilbarger_county_PR.pdf)

**What changes the search**

- Direct Wilbarger market evidence.
- Air-cooled Texas design reduces dependence on consumptive cooling water.
- AES example includes project development and long-term supply/management agreements.

**Analyst inference**

- Hinds 110 has a stronger regional comparator than a generic rural Texas site, but Google already has a controlled Wilbarger project; spare expansion land at that project may dominate any new proposal.
- The differentiating product is a transferable land-plus-energy package with construction readiness and a usable heat-rejection design.
- Avoid treating co-located clean generation as firm 24/7 deliverability or assuming Google wants another nearby site.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: Only 20–30 MW and no substantiated regional workload or expansion role.
- Inference: New site loses to existing controlled-campus capacity.
- Inference: Generation rights, interconnection or energy-management benefits do not transfer.

**Questions to validate**

- Is additional independent Wilbarger land needed, or is existing campus inventory sufficient?
- Would an expansion parcel or integrated generation-backed site be considered?
- Which facility load, firm service date and non-water cooling design are required?

**Transaction route:** Owned development with strategic energy/development partners; no evidence of willingness to acquire Bill’s option or pay a specific assignment fee.

**Acquisition signal:** Active regional construction confirmed; no open Hinds-specific or general land requisition found.

**Search brief:** Start with adjoining expansion opportunities or utility-backed nodes that improve Google’s already disclosed Texas/Iowa/Ohio/Oklahoma geography, with a written reason the existing campus cannot supply the same service.

Combines new development with acquisition and densification of network-rich existing buildings. It has a tangible preference for strategically important carrier ecosystems, not merely raw capacity.

- Both greenfield and existing-building acquisition paths verified.

- Does H5 currently favor carrier-hotel acquisitions, powered shells or greenfield sites in this market?
- What minimum reusable electrical/cooling condition is required?
- Can a phased land/building option be assigned into an H5 JV?

**Transaction route:** Direct building/land acquisitions, joint-venture capital and powered-shell/build-to-suit offerings; no option-assignment policy publicly established.

**Acquisition signal:** 2026 portfolio purchase confirms active acquisition activity; an identified open greenfield land mandate remains unknown.

### 58. Hut 8

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Energy-led AI

**Parent / shared interests:** Hut 8 Corp.; distinguish AI campus program from American Bitcoin exposure

Power-first greenfield developer that secures land and power, signs an anchor offtake, then builds phased AI infrastructure. Its own published sequence makes customer commercialization a distinct gate after technical site readiness.

**Geography:** Texas; Louisiana

**Scale:** River Bend: 330 MW utility supporting 245 MW commercialized IT; 627-acre footprint plus 2,361 acres under option. Beacon Point: 1,000 MW utility on 525 acres. Project examples, not published minimums.

**Published evidence**

- **published strategy / product:** River Bend publishes a sequence of development, anchor-tenant commercialization, construction and delivery. [Source](https://www.hut8.com/data-centers/river-bend)
- **project evidence:** Beacon Point advertises a closed-loop design with no municipal cooling-water consumption and pre-funded utility infrastructure. [Source](https://www.hut8.com/data-centers/beacon-point)
- **project evidence:** River Bend separately identifies current footprint and land under option, demonstrating phased control rather than immediate ownership of all future acreage. [Source](https://www.hut8.com/data-centers/river-bend)

**What changes the search**

- Options are demonstrably part of campus expansion control.
- Explicit utility-to-IT capacity distinction prevents overselling nominal MW.
- Generation, engineering and construction partnerships are built into delivery.

**Analyst inference**

- A new site should allow large multi-phase growth and preserve future infrastructure corridors.
- Tenant credit and bankable offtake are central; suitable power alone is insufficient to trigger construction.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: land cannot accommodate expansion or independent infrastructure.
- Inference: anchor credit or tenant demand remains unfinanceable.
- Inference: utility or permit timetable exceeds enforceable control.

**Questions to validate**

- What minimum first-phase IT block is commercially relevant for a new campus?
- Would you acquire or nominate an existing option, and what diligence remains your responsibility?
- Which customer credit and utility milestones must precede construction approval?

**Transaction route:** Land ownership/options with tenant leases and institutional delivery partners. This demonstrates use of options, not a commitment to pay an intermediary’s assignment spread.

**Acquisition signal:** Recent Texas and Louisiana projects demonstrate active commercialization. Scale and existing pipeline make a permanently limited 20 MW site a weak match absent a special customer.

**Search brief:** Source contiguous industrial/power-capable campuses with legally secure expansion acreage, reliable utility delivery, realistic cooling and generation setbacks, and a clear tenant-compatible schedule. Offer a staged control plan whose term survives studies and customer negotiation. Do not present 1 GW as a casual project request when the actual first phase is smaller.

### 59. IREN

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Energy-led AI

Vertically integrated AI cloud and data-center platform transitioning existing mining infrastructure and building large new campuses. Its disclosed US growth concentrates on large land/power positions, with customer contracts and GPU financing progressing together.

**Geography:** Texas; Oklahoma; British Columbia; Australia; Spain

**Scale:** Childress: 750 MW site and four 50 MW IT Horizon deployments. Sweetwater: 2,000 MW across 2,200 acres; Oklahoma 1,600 MW over 2,000 acres. Site MW should not be treated as commissioned IT MW.

**Published evidence**

- **published strategy / product:** IREN states its data centers are grid-connected for continuous reliable service, with renewable energy or renewable certificates. [Source](https://www.iren.com/data-centers)
- **project evidence:** FY26 results describe liquid-cooled 50 MW IT deployments at Childress, plus expansion across a pipeline exceeding 5 GW. [Source](https://irisenergy.gcs-web.com/news-releases/news-release-details/iren-reports-fy26-results)

- Need exceptionally scalable, controllable land and utility agreements rather than a one-off small plot.

- Inference: no expansion path beyond a small first phase.
- Inference: interruptible-only service fails continuous AI requirements.
- Inference: tariff, collateral or fiber costs erase the cost advantage.

**Questions to validate**

- Are you adding US sites or primarily deploying at the currently controlled pipeline?
- What evidence makes a small standalone site strategically additive?
- Which grid approvals and utility agreements must be assignable before you engage?

**Transaction route:** Own/control large sites and develop or convert infrastructure. Public material does not provide an option-assignment acceptance policy or a standard minimum land price.

**Acquisition signal:** Active development in Texas and Oklahoma, but extensive existing pipeline creates a strong alternative to buying another small site.

**Search brief:** Search expandable US land positions with executed utility arrangements or a clear path to them, robust network access, and financeable phased liquid-cooling design. For a 20–30 MW candidate, first ask whether it serves a unique customer or network need; do not infer interest from IREN’s legacy smaller Canadian sites.

### 60. Iron Mountain Data Centers

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

Compliant enterprise and hyperscale colocation, with both dense metropolitan buildings and longer-term campus growth. Location value can come from gateway connectivity and customer compliance needs rather than maximum acreage.

**Geography:** Miami; Northern Virginia; Other established Iron Mountain markets

**Scale:** Miami MIA-1 example: 3.4 acres, 150,000 square feet, 16 MW customer IT backed by 26 MVA redundant operational power. Manassas example: 142-acre master-planned campus.

[Source](https://lpc.com/local-news/lincoln-property-company-gigabit-fiber-and-tradition-holdings-team-up-to-develop-131-acre-data-center-campus-in-dallas/)
- **published strategy / product:** Lincoln’s data-center practice explicitly provides acquisitions/investments, site selection, build-to-suit development, fiber procurement and power acquisition/distribution. [Source](https://lpc.com/what-we-do/property-types/data-centers/)

**What changes the search**

- Direct precedent for land contribution plus developer financing.
- Can structure across real estate and infrastructure rather than only buy a finished center.

**Analyst inference**

- A land contribution or JV can be more aligned with Lincoln’s evidenced structures than assuming a simple option flip.
- The valuable input is entitled, connected land with a specific capital plan; bare acreage without control or an execution role is weaker.
- Dallas cluster proximity and fiber partnership were part of the Red Oak thesis. Rural Texas cannot be substituted without a new customer / network case.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: No unique site contribution beyond a marked-up listing.
- Inference: Entitlements or fiber rights cannot transfer.
- Inference: Founder cannot fund/control the position through partner review.

**Questions to validate**

- Would Lincoln evaluate an assignable option or require land ownership before a JV?
- Which markets and campus scales are currently capital-approved?
- What entitlement, power and fiber milestone must the land partner deliver?

**Transaction route:** Acquisition, investment, build-to-suit and JV/land contribution demonstrated. No guarantee it buys unexercised options; economic participation and liabilities require negotiation.

**Acquisition signal:** 2025 Red Oak transaction is clear partnership evidence, but its land is already controlled and is not an available opportunity.

**Search brief:** Search metro-adjacent entitled campuses with fiber partnership potential and a staged utility plan; prepare a land/control contribution case that explains how Lincoln’s capital unlocks a specific customer project.

1 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

### 66. Meta

**Role:** Self-build hyperscaler · **Lane:** Owner-builders

**Parent / shared interests:** Meta Platforms

Large AI campuses with utility and generation partnerships. A published electric-cooperative RFI supplies unusually specific buyer requirements and is more useful than inferring a target from a generic data-center factor list.

**Geography:** United States; Electric-cooperative territories; Louisiana; South Carolina; Wisconsin

**Scale:** RFI: path to 540 MW+ reliable scalable energy by 2030, typically 500+ acres with expansion. Source does not label the 540 MW as IT load. Requirements belong to this co-op program, not necessarily every Meta project.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** The indexed official co-op RFI asks for 540 MW+ by 2030 and typically 500+ acres with expansion potential. [Source](https://datacenters.atmeta.com/energy-co-op/)
- **published requirement:** The RFI also requests ability to bring new grid-connected resources online, renewable opportunities, closed-loop cooling water, community support and construction/operations workforce. [Source](https://datacenters.atmeta.com/energy-co-op/)
- **project evidence:** Meta’s energy page describes funding grid upgrades and new resources, including clean-energy and nuclear agreements. [Source](https://datacenters.atmeta.com/energy/)

**What changes the search**

- Explicit dated energy delivery target and land scale.
- Electric-cooperative program is a distinct procurement lane.
- RFI appears in official search index, but direct page retrieval returned 404; current intake availability must be reconfirmed.

**Analyst inference**

- Hinds 110 alone is below this RFI’s typical land envelope; adequate 20–30 MW capacity would not cure the scale mismatch.
- A co-op-backed assemblage and credible generation plan are much better search starting points than low-priced isolated acreage.
- The site must preserve control over contiguous expansion land and environmental buffers, not just gross acres.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: Standalone 110 acres / 20–30 MW offering pitched as satisfying this RFI.
- Inference: No cooperative or other credible utility-led delivery plan.
- Inference: Assemblage cannot be controlled on one realistic schedule.

**Questions to validate**

- Is the co-op RFI currently accepting responses?
- Does a staged 540 MW plan qualify and what is the first energization block?
- Can the land be optioned/assembled by a third party and what transfer/reliance rights are required?

**Transaction route:** Direct campus development and utility/generation partnerships. No evidence Meta purchases small speculative purchase-option assignments.

**Acquisition signal:** Specific public co-op solicitation indexed as of review; intake status not confirmed because direct access failed. This is not a response or interest in any candidate.

**Search brief:** Search 500+ contiguous-acre co-op service areas with a path to 540 MW+ by 2030, expandable transmission/generation, water and workforce; smaller neighboring tracts only as demonstrable campus expansions.

### 67. Microsoft

**Role:** Self-build hyperscaler and third-party capacity customer · **Lane:** Owner-builders

**Parent / shared interests:** Microsoft

Develops a geographically distributed cloud and AI estate. Its own site-selection disclosure emphasizes natural-hazard screening, economical energy and reliable telecommunications; new AI designs also alter the cooling-water requirement.

**Geography:** United States; Wisconsin; Texas; Washington; Virginia

**Scale:** No universal public land acreage or MW minimum established in reviewed sources. Avoid converting a large announced Microsoft campus into a minimum for every Azure region.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** Microsoft’s site-selection assessment screens floods, earthquakes, hurricanes and other natural hazards, alongside low-cost energy and reliable telecom infrastructure. [Source](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/compliance/assurance/assurance-datacenter-environmental-safeguards)
- **published requirement:** Microsoft says utilities map water, wastewater and pressure needs from the beginning and that Microsoft funds required infrastructure. [Source](https://local.microsoft.com/blog/understanding-water-use-at-microsoft-datacenters/)
- **project evidence:** Its newer direct-to-chip closed-loop design avoids evaporation; existing sites use several cooling designs, so zero-water design is not universal to its estate. [Source](https://local.microsoft.com/blog/understanding-water-use-at-microsoft-datacenters/)

**What changes the search**

- Explicit natural-disaster minimization policy.
- Utility-funded-by-customer infrastructure and community resource impacts matter even with ample headline power.

**Analyst inference**

- The written hazard screen should be a separate veto document, rather than a low weight within a broad score.
- Seek sites with a costed heat-rejection design: an air-cooled AI design can lower water dependence while increasing electrical and equipment needs.
- A short nominal distance to fiber is insufficient; the site must fit an Azure region’s fault-domain separation and actual network routing, which are not publicly specified.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: Uncurable natural-hazard exposure.
- Inference: Unverified region/fault-domain location.
- Inference: Power capacity is adequate but price, collateral or network construction makes total service economics unattractive.

**Questions to validate**

- Is this region seeking owned land, a build-to-suit lease or third-party capacity?
- Which fault-domain distances and fiber-path diversity tests apply?
- Which cooling design and utility customer obligations must the acquisition case support?

**Transaction route:** Self-build acquisitions and leases through specialist developers; the actual contracting counterparty depends on project. No public generic option-assignment buying policy found.

**Acquisition signal:** Published operating/design commitments are current, but neither source solicits land or identifies an open US procurement window.

**Search brief:** Find expandable industrial parcels within a validated Azure network region; pre-screen flood, seismic and storm exposure and provide a separate utility construction-cost schedule and cooling design.

1 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

Purpose-built Western campuses with water-free cooling and a high-density AI proposition. Land matching needs to consider climate, dry heat rejection, fiber and campus efficiency together; abundant water is not its defining differentiator.

Hyperscale AI/HPC campuses with liquid-cooling capability, flexible tenancy and on-site substations. Austin materials emphasize the cost and network relationship to other major Texas markets.

- Which cooling, gallery and customer-privacy dimensions must be preserved in the site layout?
- What current customer requirement would justify adding another Texas campus?

### 76. QTS

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** Blackstone / QTS

Build large, expandable power-ready campuses around long-term customer capacity planning, while integrating local government, energy suppliers, construction workforce and sustainability commitments.

**Geography:** U.S. hyperscale markets; Cedar Rapids, Iowa; Northern Virginia; Texas

**Scale:** Cedar Rapids example: 612 acres and seven planned buildings. No universal MW or acreage minimum is disclosed in the cited criteria.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** QTS expressly names sustainability, tax, permitting and labor in its strategic site-selection process for large-scale power-ready campuses. [Source](https://q.com/why-qts/access-to-infrastructure/)
- **project evidence:** Cedar Rapids campus occupies 612 acres within Big Cedar Industrial Center, with Alliant Energy collaboration and non-evaporative cooling. [Source](https://q.com/news/qts-announces-new-world-class-data-center-campus-in-cedar-rapids/)

**What changes the search**

- Labor and permitting appear in the company’s own selection criteria.
- Campus partnerships combine utilities, local government and long-term customer growth.

**Analyst inference**

- Regional workforce depth and the ability to execute several buildings over time should be screened before an option is priced. Cheap acres with weak civil access or contractor capacity may not solve the campus delivery problem.
- Look for a local economic-development partner able to coordinate infrastructure and predictable approvals, while verifying incentive eligibility and obligations rather than treating a headline tax exemption as cash.
- A package should show phased expansion geometry, substation siting and community-impact buffers; isolated capacity with no expansion pathway is a weaker match.

- Inference: large power headline unsupported by a long-term campus expansion plan.
- Inference: severe labor or community opposition that destroys the delivery timetable despite available MW.

**Questions to validate**

- Which regions have unsatisfied tenant demand outside your existing land bank?
- How much initial capacity and how many later phases are needed?
- Which tax, workforce and community commitments must be solved before land investment approval?

**Transaction route:** Development and land control are clearly evidenced; exact willingness to purchase contract rights or pay an originator is not publicly established. Approach the acquisition/development function with a controlled opportunity.

**Search brief:** Source contiguous industrial-campus sites in regions with credible utility cooperation, scalable construction labor and room for phased substations and buffers. Add a community and workforce plan to the usual technical checklist.

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Energy-led AI

**Parent / shared interests:** Riot Platforms Inc.

A bitcoin-origin infrastructure owner now demonstrating actual leased AI data-center development. The most relevant acquisition evidence concerns gaining fee ownership beneath an existing powered campus, not indiscriminate greenfield expansion.

**Scale:** AMD initial 25 MW critical IT, with expansion rights up to 200 MW; separate 191 MW critical-IT frontier-lab lease. Existing portfolio headline power is utility capacity, not all operating AI IT.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Riot purchased 200 acres beneath its occupied Rockdale site for $96 million and signed an initial AMD lease in January 2026. [Source](https://www.riotplatforms.com/riot-announces-fee-simple-acquisition-of-land-and-first-data-center-lease-with-amd-at-the-rockdale-site/)
- **project evidence:** The first AMD deployment used retrofit of an existing building and included explicit expansion options/rights. [Source](https://www.riotplatforms.com/riot-announces-fee-simple-acquisition-of-land-and-first-data-center-lease-with-amd-at-the-rockdale-site/)
- **project evidence:** August 2026 results reported 241 MW contracted critical IT across AMD and another tenant, using existing approved Rockdale interconnection. [Source](https://www.riotplatforms.com/riot-platforms-reports-second-quarter-2026-financial-results-and-strategic-highlights/)

**What changes the search**

- Fee title can be a prerequisite to financeable hyperscale leasing even when a company already operates on the land.
- Retrofit economics and ready infrastructure are important advantages.
- Customer expansion rights can reserve future capacity without immediate construction.

**Analyst inference**

- Prioritize land or rights that unlock an already powered campus; a generic rural site is less compelling than control that removes a known leasing obstacle.
- A 25 MW first phase is possible, but the proven example includes substantial expansion and existing infrastructure.
- Competing internal conversion opportunities may make new-site acquisitions unnecessary despite market demand.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: no advantage over internally controlled approved power.
- Inference: site cannot support tenant expansion rights.
- Inference: control structure prevents financeable long-term leasing.

**Questions to validate**

- Are you evaluating new external campuses or focusing on approved existing sites?
- What real-estate rights must be fee owned?
- Would a small retrofit with rapid delivery be considered outside the Texas platform?

**Transaction route:** Fee-simple acquisition, modified-gross leases and customer expansion rights are evidenced. No public promise to acquire standalone small options found.

**Acquisition signal:** Now an evidenced AI developer rather than mining-only prospect; new external land appetite remains less certain than existing-campus growth.

**Search brief:** Search assets that offer reliable energized service, adaptable buildings or an expansion/control problem Riot can solve economically. For standalone raw land, require a demonstrable advantage over Rockdale/Corsicana before seeking an option. A small block alone should not be represented as aligned merely because AMD began at 25 MW.

- **project evidence:** Austin specifications offer flexible redundancy configurations, diverse long-haul fiber and connection to major Texas hubs. [Source](https://www.skyboxdatacenters.com/locations/powercampus-austin)

**What changes the search**

- Current Wichita Falls precedent makes a relevant regional comparator.
- Sub-3 ms Infomart positioning is a commercial benchmark rather than generic connectivity.

**Analyst inference**

- Hinds should not be rejected solely for distance from Dallas, because Skybox markets a Wichita Falls campus. It should still be compared against that existing, larger, network-positioned competitor.
- The 300 MW-plus PowerCampus product is materially larger than a 20–50 MW Hinds hypothesis. Do not assume the firm will accept a small site because it is regionally nearby.
- Exact route latency to Infomart, entitlement certainty and privately buildable substation land are buyer-specific validation tasks beyond generic fiber/power availability.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: a 20–50 MW terminal site does not match its published 300 MW-plus PowerCampus offer.
- Inference: purported regional latency cannot be supported by a constructible diverse route.

**Questions to validate**

- Would any current Skybox product take a 20–50 MW site, or is 300 MW-plus the relevant floor?
- What maximum routed latency to Infomart is required outside DFW?
- Does Wichita Falls satisfy your regional demand, or is additional acquisition capacity needed?

**Transaction route:** Land/development partnerships are evidenced, including project-specific sponsors. Exact willingness to buy an outside option and minimum consideration remain unverified.

**Acquisition signal:** Wichita Falls is controlled Skybox supply, not a new acquisition request. Its claims require utility/route verification before using it as an engineering comparable.

**Search brief:** Search large North/Central Texas sites with by-right use, private-substation geometry and measured fiber latency to major hubs. Smaller leads require a separate turnkey or expansion mandate confirmed by Skybox.

2 researched site hypotheses; no expressed buyer interest verified.

### 84. Soluna

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Energy-led AI

**Parent / shared interests:** Soluna Holdings; Metrobloks co-development at Kati 2—shared project demand

Renewable-powered compute developer with distinct Bitcoin and AI programs. Its specialty is monetizing constrained renewable energy, but AI campuses need a different reliability and commercial structure from flexible mining loads.

**Geography:** Texas; Renewable-rich US power markets

**Scale:** Kati 2 proposal: 100+ MW critical IT initial AI phase, expansion roadmap above 300 MW critical IT. Dorothy legacy 50 MW and 48 MW projects are mining, not proof of AI suitability.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** January 2026 MOU describes Kati 2 co-development: Soluna contributes site control, power entitlements and equipment; Metrobloks leads design, leasing and operations. [Source](https://www.solunacomputing.com/news/metrobloks-project-kati/)
- **project evidence:** The initial Kati 2 tenant interest was a nonbinding LOI and the parties intended to source third-party capital together. [Source](https://www.solunacomputing.com/news/metrobloks-project-kati/)
- **project evidence:** Company portfolio separately classifies operating Bitcoin facilities and planned AI developments, including Dorothy 3. [Source](https://www.solunacomputing.com/projects/)

**What changes the search**

- Generator economics and curtailment reduction are part of the commercial proposition.
- Co-development splits power/site origination from AI building and customer responsibilities.
- Nonbinding tenant interest is materially weaker than a signed lease.

**Analyst inference**

- Best search locations have a specific renewable generator relationship and controllable grid/behind-the-meter architecture, not merely regional wind resources.
- An AI match requires firm service and an operations partner capable of meeting the tenant SLA.
- Small rural land can fit a renewable compute use without being a good AI data-center exit; keep those buyer lanes separate.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: only intermittent surplus energy with no firm AI service solution.
- Inference: generator has no incentive or authority to contract.
- Inference: lease/financing remains speculative while founder bears studies.

**Questions to validate**

- Would you evaluate a 20–50 MW site for AI, mining, or neither?
- What generator arrangement and curtailment profile produce an investable project?
- Which rights and documents would Soluna versus its AI development partner require?

**Transaction route:** Project-company co-development, infrastructure contribution and third-party capital are evidenced. A founder-controlled land option may be an input, but no standard option-acquisition policy is public.

**Acquisition signal:** Active AI development intent alongside operational mining assets; project pipeline should not be counted as commissioned AI capacity.

**Search brief:** Search near renewable assets with curtailment economics, a workable load service arrangement, expansion acreage and evidence of reliable AI-compatible supply. Test a 20–50 MW renewable compute block separately from a 100+ MW AI campus. Ask whether the developer wants a land-only position, power rights or an integrated generator partnership.

No defensible marketed match established in this run. No sufficiently specific marketed match found in this run. The search brief below remains actionable; buyer geography, product and unmet capacity need must be confirmed before attaching a parcel.

### 85. STACK Infrastructure

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** STACK / Blue Owl investment relationship

Large-scale hyperscale development with customer and capital alignment. Project Jupiter demonstrates an integrated energy-and-data-center route where a dedicated microgrid is part of delivering AI capacity.

**Geography:** New Mexico; Dallas-Fort Worth; Northern Virginia; Phoenix; Atlanta; Chicago; Portland; Silicon Valley

**Scale:** New Mexico precedent exceeds 1 GW planned capacity; source does not unambiguously distinguish utility power from critical IT for that headline. No universal parcel threshold found.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** STACK’s September 2025 announcement links its planned Doña Ana County campus to Oracle/Stargate demand, an on-site microgrid and low-water closed-loop cooling. [Source](https://www.stackinfra.com/about/news-press/press-releases/stack-infrastructure-delivers-digital-infrastructure-for-data-center-campus-in-new-mexico/)
- **project evidence:** STACK and BorderPlex pledged water/community investment and said Project Jupiter would use dedicated generation to meet its own energy needs. - A power-generation partner, fuel/air-permit path and customer-backed infrastructure schedule can matter as much as the acreage. A raw tract beside a gas pipeline does not recreate the announced microgrid model.
- Identify the actual project sponsor and committed customer function early. STACK and a funding partner are not independent takeout probabilities for the same transaction.
- Community investment and local infrastructure obligations must enter the land economics, not be left as a later public-relations exercise.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: energy concept is only a pipeline or retired-plant proximity claim.
- Inference: project lacks a credible tenant/capital sponsor while requiring major generation investment.

**Questions to validate**

- Does the current mandate accept behind-the-meter generation and what emissions/reliability standards apply?
- Is STACK the land buyer, developer for a tenant, or JV operator in this market?
- Which infrastructure and community obligations must transfer with the site?

**Transaction route:** Development partnerships are evidenced. A proposal may need to become part of a sponsored project rather than a simple land-option flip; no standard cash assignment terms are published.

**Acquisition signal:** Project Jupiter is an announced controlled development with specified partners. It does not establish an open land solicitation or capacity at neighboring parcels.

**Search brief:** Search either scalable established-market campuses or integrated industrial/energy sites with documented customer utility. For the latter, screen generation permitting, gas supply and local water/community obligations alongside land title.

### 86. Stream Data Centers

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** Stream Data Centers

Proactive development where future demand, total cost, low risk, connectivity and sustainability align; offers powered shells, wholesale colocation and build-to-suit solutions.

**Geography:** San Antonio; Dallas; Phoenix; Chicago; Atlanta

**Scale:** San Antonio examples range from a 33-acre/30 MW critical-capacity powered shell to a 200 MW campus with a 334 MW CPS substation. Substation rating and delivered IT capacity are not interchangeable.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** Stream expressly identifies total cost, low risk, robust network connectivity and achievable sustainability as its location strategy. [Source](https://www.streamdatacenters.com/locations/san-antonio-data-centers/)
- **project evidence:** San Antonio campus materials describe 200 MW ultimate development, a 334 MW on-site substation and a fully leased initial building. [Source](https://www.streamdatacenters.com/locations/san-antonio-data-centers/)

**What changes the search**

- The company articulates a location strategy beyond MW.
- Multiple product types permit smaller historical examples without establishing a new small-site buying mandate.

**Analyst inference**

- The effective search product is a low-total-cost location with a demonstrated customer story. Purchase price alone is a poor ranking variable if civil work, fiber or utility upgrades eliminate the advantage.
- A parcel near a known submarket may be useful, but must offer incremental timing or economics compared with Stream’s controlled land and available space.
- A reusable industrial property can fit the powered-shell lane when structure, floor loads, clear height and physical infrastructure justify the conversion; this is a separate screen from raw-campus land.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: headline inexpensive land but costly infrastructure/civil delivery.
- Inference: no advantage over Stream’s available buildings or controlled expansion land.

**Questions to validate**

- Is the current requirement a powered shell, turnkey campus or customer-led build-to-suit?
- Which cost, delivery or customer-location gap would justify another parcel in this market?
- Could control transfer before utility construction, and who funds remaining infrastructure?

**Transaction route:** Development, build-to-suit and prior facility sales are evidenced. Third-party option-assignment purchase terms remain unknown.

**Acquisition signal:** San Antonio materials show both fully leased development and currently marketed capacity. Existing supply must be considered before asserting unmet local demand.

**Search brief:** Search in disclosed metros for low-cost, low-risk campus land or a conversion property with a quantified delivery advantage. Attach a comparative total-development-cost estimate and carrier-route evidence.

### 88. Switch

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

Large regional exascale campuses and network ecosystems, with a repeatable zero-consumptive-cooling design. Its own large land and infrastructure position raises the hurdle for an unrelated small tract.

- Express forward-only EVO design policy.
- Regional latency and campus ecosystems distinguish it from a generic rural compute developer.

**Acquisition signal:** Existing campus expansion is evidence of construction activity and also competing land supply. Renewable-energy marketing should not be mistaken for parcel-specific utility availability.

- **project evidence:** Grayslake selection emphasized the connection between northern Chicago and southeastern Wisconsin, tax incentives, labor, connectivity and power; the site was already zoned for data centers. **Acquisition signal:** Grayslake and Palmetto are controlled developments; their future-phase dates are company targets and not verification of new parcel demand.

**Search brief:** Search substantial already-entitled campus sites in confirmed target corridors with staged substation delivery. A 20 MW lead should not be ranked highly without a specific exception or customer mandate.

### 90. TA Digital Group / TA Realty

**Role:** hyperscale build-to-suit developer/investment platform · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** TA Realty; TA Digital Group is its development arm, not an independent buyer

Builds for hyperscale tenants in Tier 1 US markets and monetizes completed stabilized assets. A prospective land option must support financeable tenant delivery and eventual asset sale, not simply a promising technical use.

**Geography:** Northern Virginia; Chicago; Atlanta

**Scale:** Published product ranges: 20–100 MW strategic infill in mature markets and 100 MW–1 GW campuses. MW basis is not specified on the solutions page. The 2026 Leesburg sale separately identifies 165 MW IT load in two completed buildings.

**Published evidence**

- **published requirement:** TA expressly describes its strategy as build-to-suit hyperscale facilities in Tier 1 US markets followed by monetization on stabilization. [Source](https://www.tarealty.com/news/ta-realty-announces-sale-of-hyperscale-data-centers-in-northern-virginia)
- **project evidence:** The Leesburg project assembled land, added utility infrastructure and provided two independent substations; platform includes Chicago and Atlanta. [Source](https://www.tarealty.com/news/ta-realty-announces-sale-of-hyperscale-data-centers-in-northern-virginia)
- **published requirement:** TA Digital explicitly separates 20–100 MW mature-market low-latency infill from 100 MW–1 GW campuses, with build-to-suit, turnkey, powered-shell and fee-development choices. [Source](https://www.tadigitalgroup.com/solutions)

**What changes the search**

- Stabilized sale is part of its stated business model.
- Two independent substations in the disclosed flagship are a relevant design precedent, not a promise that every site requires exactly two.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: match either a 20–100 MW low-latency infill product or a larger single-tenant/multi-building campus. Do not exclude modest acreage if it is a strong mature-market infill position.
- Inference: easements and parcel geometry must preserve separate substation access and building phasing.
- Inference: a small rural tract outside a Tier 1 market has low strategic relevance even with adequate MW.
- Inference: underwriting must compare all-in delivered cost with alternatives already in the platform; cheap dirt is a small input to institutional asset economics.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: no Tier 1 customer requirement or no path to institutional-scale stabilization.
- Inference: single critical utility corridor cannot satisfy the target tenant redundancy configuration.

**Questions to validate**

- What land size and MW delivery schedule fit the next tenant requirement rather than your existing campus inventory?
- Can a third-party option be acquired at a defined diligence milestone or do you originate all land contracts internally?

**Transaction route:** Build-to-suit, turnkey, powered shell and fee development are published offerings. Institutional land assembly and stabilized asset sale are evidenced; a fee-development offering does not establish that TA will pay an originator an assignment fee.

**Acquisition signal:** 2026 disposal release reports more than 12 owned/controlled projects and nearly 3 GW, establishing an active platform but also substantial competing inventory.

**Search brief:** Run two searches: compact infill sites in mature network ecosystems for the published 20–100 MW lane, and larger expandable Tier 1 campuses for the 100 MW–1 GW lane. Specify which product a prospect serves before considering land price. Validate tenant geography and independent utility approaches.

### 92. TeraWulf

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Energy-led AI

**Parent / shared interests:** TeraWulf Inc.; historical Abernathy JV with Fluidstack was announced for sale in 2026

Vertically integrated power-led developer whose strongest current pattern is industrial or generation-site reuse with substantial electrical infrastructure and long-duration AI leases. It prioritizes control of infrastructure and customer economics, rather than accumulating undeveloped MW claims.

**Geography:** New York; Kentucky; Maryland

**Scale:** Justified campus approximately 401 MW critical IT; Hawesville acquisition described more than 250 buildable acres and up to 480 MW gross power. These are project examples.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Lake Mariner reused a retired coal-power site; Hawesville is a former industrial site with existing high-voltage infrastructure. [Source](https://investors.terawulf.com/sec-filings/all-sec-filings/content/0001083301-26-000092/wulf-20260331.htm)
- **project evidence:** July 2026 Anthropic agreement covered approximately 401 MW critical IT over 20 years, with phased delivery planned in 2027–2028. [Source](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1083301/000110465926080583/tm2619468d1_ex99-1.htm)
- **project evidence:** The company announced a sale of its majority Abernathy JV interest to recycle capital into wholly owned projects. [Source](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1083301/000110465926080583/tm2619468d1_ex99-1.htm)

**What changes the search**

- Generation-site redevelopment experience.
- Preference for direct control of infrastructure/customer relationships evidenced by capital recycling.
- Long-lived anchor leases make energization schedule and tenant credit central commercial factors.

**Analyst inference**

- A brownfield with transferable service rights and completed cleanup is more aligned than an agricultural site merely crossed by transmission.
- Contaminated legacy assets can fit only if liability, remediation schedule and buildable areas are tightly bounded.
- Partner capacity and credit support remain necessary even after power is proven; infrastructure ownership alone does not establish a paying tenant.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: prior generation export rights are mistaken for large-load import rights.
- Inference: cleanup or title liabilities cannot be ring-fenced.
- Inference: small final campus fails to justify a separate operating platform.

**Questions to validate**

- Would a small first phase be considered if the site can ultimately reach several hundred MW?
- What environmental indemnities and remediation completion tests do you require?
- Do you prefer fee title, a long ground lease, or project-company acquisition?

**Transaction route:** Direct property purchases and project-company/JV ownership are evidenced. Neither history proves a willingness to acquire unexercised options or assume unlimited environmental obligations.

**Acquisition signal:** New industrial-site acquisitions and contracted Kentucky development support current acquisition relevance. The minimum acceptable standalone campus is unpublished.

**Search brief:** Search remediated power or industrial properties with large net buildable acreage, documented import-service rights, existing substation potential and room for redundant equipment. Compare a redevelopment schedule against greenfield—not just nominal installed generation. Founder should avoid taking environmental or demolition obligations before a funded sponsor accepts them.

### 93. TierPoint

**Role:** Enterprise developer/operator and campus acquirer · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** TierPoint platform; financing ownership not reverified

Enterprise colocation transitioning into larger AI deployments, with strong acquisition-and-expansion precedent. It can value existing customer ecosystems and operational infrastructure as much as vacant acreage.

**Geography:** United States; Eastern Pennsylvania; Dallas/Fort Worth TX; St Louis MO

**Scale:** TekPark 137 acre campus with 100 MW power expansion; Fort Worth acquired 208,000 sq ft with 5 MW available, 8 MW near-term and 18 MW long-term plans. These are project figures; IT / facility basis not consistently specified.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** October 2025 announcement describes buying its formerly leased TekPark campus and funding 100 MW expansion targeted for second-half 2026. [Source](https://www.tierpoint.com/news/tierpoint-completes-240-million-securitization-financing-and-acquisition-of-pennsylvania-data-center-and-campus/)
- **project evidence:** Fort Worth 2024 acquisition offered staged 5/8/18 MW and both conventional colocation and GPU/high-density deployment use cases. [Source](https://www.tierpoint.com/news/tierpoint-acquires-new-dallas-area-data-center/)

**What changes the search**

- Strong proof of acquiring a leased operating campus.
- Both 5 MW entry and 100 MW campus expansion examples.
- Demand-backed high-density cooling appears more important than a universal rack-density rule.

**Analyst inference**

- A modest first phase with a practical campus ramp can fit; a 100 MW end-state is not required for every TierPoint transaction.
- Look for former enterprise facilities or land near existing TierPoint demand where reuse shortens delivery.
- Expanding an occupied leased campus reduces customer-migration risk; a new rural greenfield site must overcome that advantage.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: No customer case beyond cheap megawatts.
- Inference: Expansion requires replacing most existing infrastructure without a time advantage.
- Inference: New region loses to current campus expansion.

**Questions to validate**

- Is there a power/space shortage in one of your existing markets?
- Could an initial 5–10 MW campus with documented expansion fit?
- Would you prefer acquiring an operating facility or a new build-to-suit?

**Transaction route:** Fee-simple acquisitions of facilities/campuses and financed expansion. No public generic assignment-fee criteria found.

**Acquisition signal:** Recent financed TekPark acquisition and Fort Worth expansion demonstrate acquisition appetite, but not interest in a specific marketed tract.

**Search brief:** Prioritize existing regional enterprise facilities with expansion land, useful plant and identified large customer demand; record cost to migrate or retain tenants and deploy liquid cooling.

### 94. Tract

**Role:** master developer of powered and entitled land · **Lane:** Land / campus infrastructure

**Parent / shared interests:** Tract platform; separate from Quinbrook-backed Rowan

Creates infrastructure-ready parks and sells a development position to hyperscalers and wholesale developers. This is a land/infrastructure buyer and potential competitor to Syncranicity; it is not automatically the eventual building operator.

**Geography:** Texas; Arizona; Nevada; Utah; Virginia; Iowa; Illinois

**Scale:** Observed parks range from the 343-acre Morris acquisition to roughly 3,000 acres at Caldwell Valley; published ultimate loads reach gigawatts. These are precedents, not a disclosed acreage minimum. Initial contracted grid blocks and ultimate marketed capacity are different figures.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Caldwell acquisitions added 1,458 contiguous acres. Its first 250 MW was contracted for Q1 2028; the much larger ultimate park includes a natural-gas/generation strategy. [Source](https://www.tract.com/news/tract-closes-on-two-incremental-acquisitions-adding-1458-acres-to-caldwell-valley-technology-park-in-caldwell-county-texas-bringing-total-campus-to-nearly-3000-acres/)
- **project evidence:** Morris was acquired already zoned/entitled, with an executed ComEd Transmission Security Agreement, initial June 2028 energization target and a phased ramp through 2032. [Source](https://www.tract.com/news/tract-enters-greater-chicago-market-with-the-acquisition-of-343-acres-to-be-developed-as-a-1gw-data-center-campus-known-as-the-morris-technology-park/)
- **published strategy / product:** Tract prepositions power, fiber, zoning and entitlements and engages communities before development. [Source](https://www.tract.com/)

**What changes the search**

- Campus-level land planning and infrastructure are the product sold.
- A contracted first energization block can coexist with a much larger, still phased ultimate program.

**Analyst inference**

- Inference: prioritize assemblages where shared substations, roads and fiber can serve multiple downstream developers; a small isolated tract needs an expansion or infill role.
- Inference: a credible multiyear land-control runway may matter more than an immediate cheap asking price because the infrastructure program is large.
- Inference: demonstrate why a parcel improves an existing target park or opens a defensible new regional node before paying for site studies.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: no credible contiguity or expansion path; parcel too small to justify shared backbone infrastructure.
- Inference: community rejection or a short option that expires before the transmission program can advance.

**Questions to validate**

- Is the acquisition team buying new regional nodes or only adjacent expansion parcels now?
- What minimum first-phase block, parcel size, control term and power security must a third-party land position have?

**Transaction route:** Documented outright acquisitions and adjacent assemblages. Willingness to acquire a third-party option or pay an assignment premium is unverified.

**Acquisition signal:** January 2026 acquisitions show continuing land aggregation; they are not an open procurement request for every US parcel.

**Search brief:** Search contiguous industrial tracts/assemblages in its disclosed corridors, with room for independent substation sites and heavy civil infrastructure. Seek written utility milestones and local development-agreement pathways. Do not treat Hinds-sized acreage as a substitute for campus-scale optionality.

### 95. US Signal

**Role:** Fiber-integrated data-center operator and expansion developer · **Lane:** Regional / interconnection

**Parent / shared interests:** Igneo Infrastructure Partners-backed (company disclosure)

Coordinates data-center expansion with its own fiber infrastructure and regional enterprise/cloud products. Existing network reach and customer access are part of the acquisition logic, not independent checklist items.

**Geography:** Midwest US; Detroit MI; Des Moines IA; Aurora IL; Oak Brook IL

**Scale:** 2025 program added 3 MW commercial power in Detroit and 6 MW in Des Moines. Aurora plans distinguish 6 MW commercial power from 4 MW UPS-supported capacity, with longer-term 8–10 MW total-facility ambition.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** US Signal announced a $200 million program with data-center additions and more than 1,000 miles of dense fiber/conduit. [Source](https://ussignal.com/press-releases/expands-ai-infrastructure/)
- **project evidence:** Aurora, acquired December 2025, is receiving UPS, generator and cooling upgrades, plus a new link to Oak Brook. [Source](https://ussignal.com/press-releases/accelertes-aurora-data-center-expansion/)
- **project evidence:** Aurora phase two explicitly separates utility/commercial capacity from UPS-supported capacity; future 8–10 MW is a target, not current capacity. [Source](https://ussignal.com/press-releases/accelertes-aurora-data-center-expansion/)

**What changes the search**

- Useful public separation of facility and UPS-supported capacity.
- Owned-network expansion is tightly coupled to data-center investments.

**Analyst inference**

- Parcels or buildings on an economic extension of its backbone rank above disconnected low-cost land.
- A 10–20 MW first phase may already be above several cited regional upgrades; secure an actual demand brief instead of assuming more MW is always better.
- Network construction, resilient IT capacity and customer migration costs should be modeled as one project.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: Network extension erases power/land savings.
- Inference: Power figure ignores UPS/cooling limits.
- Inference: No incremental customer requirement after current expansion program.

**Questions to validate**

- Where does the current network/customer pipeline require another site?
- What critical IT load and utility-feed redundancy are needed?
- Would a former enterprise center with expansion land beat existing campus expansion economics?

**Transaction route:** Facility acquisition followed by phased upgrades; no public blanket raw-land or option-assignment program.

**Acquisition signal:** June 2026 Aurora expansion is a current capital program but consumes funds and customer demand within an already owned site.

**Search brief:** Search Midwest enterprise campuses connected to US Signal routes, with room for redundant plant and a feasible path to additional critical IT load; validate demand beyond existing-campus upgrades.

### 96. Vantage Data Centers

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Hyperscale campuses

**Parent / shared interests:** Vantage Data Centers

Hyperscale and AI campuses with repeatable operating standards, substantial power blocks and very high rack densities. Its Texas expansion demonstrates willingness to build outside central metropolitan land markets when the infrastructure package works.

**Geography:** Texas; Northern Virginia; Phoenix; Other established North American markets

**Scale:** Frontier, Shackelford County: 1,200 acres, 1.4 GW critical IT, ten buildings and 250 kW-plus racks. This specific mega-campus is not a universal acquisition floor.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** Frontier specifications pair 1.4 GW IT capacity with N+1 redundancy, multiple power feeds, liquid cooling and diverse dark/lit fiber paths. [Source](https://vantage-dc.com/data-center-locations/north-america/shackelford-county-tx)
- **project evidence:** August 2025 announcement identifies rural Texas Frontier as an AI-led expansion, using low-water closed-loop cooling and targeting initial delivery in H2 2026. **What changes the search**

- Published Frontier IT load prevents confusing utility nameplate with critical compute power.
- Demonstrated rural AI-campus strategy, with high-density cooling and route diversity.

**Analyst inference**

- For a rural lead, test high-density heat rejection, diverse long-haul fiber easements and construction labor/logistics together. A utility letter alone does not show the land can support the customer’s physical layout.
- Remote geography is not automatically disqualifying, but a 20 MW isolated first phase competes against much larger controlled projects unless a specific customer requirement or faster delivery gives it a reason to exist.
- Substation expansion corridors and independently constructible fiber routes should be reserved in the site plan rather than counted as spare acreage.

**Potential rejection risks**

- Inference: only a small terminal MW block with no expansion or customer-specific mandate.
- Inference: two nominal carriers sharing the same physical corridor.

**Questions to validate**

- What minimum first-phase and ultimate IT block is needed for a new rural campus?
- Would a 20–50 MW project have a distinct customer use, or fall below your current development mandate?
- Which firm service-date, water and fiber evidence is required for land acquisition?

**Transaction route:** Developer-led campuses are evidenced; no standard third-party land-option purchase policy is published. An originator would need to sell a material timing or land-control advantage.

**Acquisition signal:** Frontier is already controlled and under development. Its published delivery date is a target, not independent verification of energization or proof of appetite for another nearby tract.

**Search brief:** Search large, assemblable Texas or other North American tracts with staged power, liquid-cooling suitability and truly diverse fiber. Only retain smaller land leads when an identified Vantage customer or campus extension supports them.

### 97. Verrus

**Role:** greenfield hyperscale developer with integrated battery-energy management · **Lane:** Energy-led AI

**Parent / shared interests:** Sidewalk Infrastructure Partners-backed; Alphabet is an investor in SIP, not an independent site-specific takeout

Builds grid-responsive campuses using on-site batteries and proprietary control systems to combine high-availability computing with flexible AI workloads. The actual utility tariff, grid-service rules and workload mix matter unusually much to its site thesis.

**Geography:** United States; specific current acquisition markets not disclosed in reviewed primary sources

**Scale:** No defensible public minimum acreage or first-phase MW found. Corporate materials advertise availability up to 99.999% where required; that is a service-design claim, not a universal load-curtailment allowance.

**Published evidence**

- **published strategy / product:** Company describes balancing grid, carbon and compute requirements, including flexible/batched AI workloads and high-availability service. [Source](https://www.verrusdata.com/)
- **project evidence:** Its primary recruitment material identifies greenfield hyperscale construction with large-scale BESS, grid services and capitalized turnkey facilities. [Source](https://www.verrusdata.com/careers/software-engineer-sre)

- Inference: no safe/permitable storage layout or customer load cannot tolerate the chosen flexibility.

- What acreage, BESS duration, first-phase MW and customer SLA must a new site accommodate?

**Transaction route:** Developer capitalizes turnkey campuses; land acquisition, JV and assignment terms not public.

**Acquisition signal:** Current engineering/community hiring and active platform support continued development. No parcel acquisition mandate was verified.

**Search brief:** Obtain its active utility/market brief first. Then search BESS-compatible industrial land where dispatch, interconnection, fire setbacks, emissions and customer service obligations can be engineered together. Avoid manufacturing a site preference from office location.

**Role:** developer/operator · **Lane:** Energy-led AI

AI cloud and colocation developer with a demonstrated industrial-building reuse model. Its most informative US precedent is a former textile facility that already had scale, electrical/cooling infrastructure and room for phased growth.

**Geography:** North Carolina; Canada; Other customer-led international deployments

**Scale:** NC-1: approximately 970,000 square feet on 96 acres in the acquisition description. August 2026 results report 40 MW contracted IT; later gross expansion ambitions must not be confused with currently delivered IT.

**Published evidence**

- **project evidence:** NC-1 acquisition materials describe retrofit of a former textile plant to Tier III standards, with an initial 24 MW phase and expansion dependent on Duke Energy agreements. [Source](https://www.whitefiber.com/blog/nc-1-data-center)
- **project evidence:** Current NC-1 specifications describe direct-to-chip cooling, 150 kW rack density and concurrently maintainable design. [Source](https://www.whitefiber.com/nc-1)

- Prioritize industrial shells with genuinely reusable electrical, structural and cooling assets; demolition-only factories do not replicate NC-1.

- Inference: utility expansion is merely aspirational.
- Inference: no customer/network need beyond already controlled NC-1 capacity.

- What minimum immediate IT capacity and expansion potential justify acquisition?

**Acquisition signal:** Operating customer deployment and current expansion demonstrate capacity demand, but new sites must compete with NC-1’s remaining growth potential.

**Search brief:** Search former mills or industrial campuses near North Carolina/other target network corridors, especially sites with documented usable electrical equipment and expansion rights. Verify the reuse value through engineering rather than using floor area alone. Ask whether WhiteFiber wants additional North Carolina capacity or a geographically distinct node before controlling another nearby property.

## Specific offerings and buyer-site scenarios

### Hinds 110

HOLD / availability and tract conflict · Low-cash discovery first

TBD FM 2072 / County Road 105 N, Vernon, TX 76384

Advertised acres: 110. Land.com and HAR displayed $385,000 on October 29. Other snapshots/archive pages show $319,000 or $352,000 and conflicting sold status. Treat price/status as unsettled until Davis Vernon confirms MLS 21317653.

Buyer-first hypothesis: Dataprana’s 10–50 MW grid strategy is closer to a possible phased 110-acre project than a generic gigawatt-campus buyer list. AIB is an exception test only if its current 40–90 MW product can be supported. Neither has reviewed Hinds.

**Availability:** Current Land.com and HAR views say For Sale at $385,000; an archived MLS page says Sold at $352,000. No seller confirmation. Listing ID 27899900 is not an APN.

**Parcel:** Unresolved. A prior watch identified parent account 100771, but it is not reconciled to this 110-acre offering. HAR legal description references ABST 2054 and ABST 165, BLK 12, SEC 30 & 31, H&TC.

**Power evidence:** Crossing 138 kV / 345 kV lines are marketing claims only. Exact distribution/transmission provider, AEP versus cooperative service path, ISO territory, load rights, tariff and schedule remain unresolved. No available MW is verified.

**Control:** Unknown. No seller has offered Syncranicity an assignable option, exclusivity, or buyer-backed control.

**Blockers**

- Reconcile active/sold/archive and price conflicts.
- Exact APN, owner, tract and access need county/title verification.
- No written power path or diverse fiber.
- Texas approval/timing and cooling questions remain.
- A 110-acre tract may be too small for a developer requiring several hundred MW and extensive expansion.
- Texas Governor’s September 21, 2026 direction pauses affected TCEQ data-center permit reviews pending safeguards. Exact permit scope and current treatment must be established; no end date was verified in this run. Do not assume power confirmation resolves permitting.

**Next proof:** Ask Davis Vernon Agency to reconcile current MLS status, asking price, exact county account/legal description and seller willingness to allow studies.

**Dataprana / Prana Energy** — NO NUMERICAL SCENARIO: No numerical scenario until current availability and the exact offered tract are reconciled.

**Parcel:** Unresolved. Exact offered parcel and legal description unresolved.

This is an acquisition/expansion comparable and partnership lead, not a cheap vacant parcel or an empty facility awaiting first construction.
